**Abstract:** We evaluate the capability of next-generation gravitational-wave detectors, including LISA, the Einstein Telescope, and DECIGO, to probe cosmographic parameters using electromagnetically bright standard sirens. We examine the constraints primarily as a function of the number of detections and, in a complementary analysis, across correlated SNR--redshift regimes. LISA provides percent-level constraints on $H_0$, while the Einstein Telescope and DECIGO reach subpercent precision as the catalogue size increases. ## 52. [Detectability of Eccentric Intermediate Mass-Ratio Inspiral in the Decihertz Band](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21323) <a id="link52"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21323

**Authors:** Devesh Giri, Sajal Mukherjee, Apratim Ganguly, Sanjit Mitra

**Abstract:** The proposed decihertz gravitational-wave (GW) band $(\sim 10^{-2}-1\,\Hz)$ will observe the late inspiral of intermediate mass-ratio inspirals (IMRIs), binaries with mass ratios $10^2 \lesssim q \lesssim 10^4$ that assemble dynamically in dense stellar environments and are expected to retain measurable eccentricity. Search and parameter-estimation pipelines are predominantly built around circular templates, and we test this assumption. Using black-hole perturbation theory waveforms from \textsc{FastEMRIWaveforms} for a representative IMRI ($M=10^4\,\modot$, $q=100$, observed for its final $10\,\hr$), we compute fitting factors between eccentric signals and circular templates, maximizing over the template's total mass and mass ratio with a differential-evolution search, for the B-DECIGO, LGWA-Si, and IndIGO-D noise curves and a flat PSD. The mismatch grows with initial eccentricity $e_0$ and crosses the standard $3\%$ threshold at $e_0\simeq3\times10^{-2}$, well within the range allowed by the waveform model and expected from dynamical formation. The best-fitting circular templates also bias the total mass and mass ratio by several percent at $e_0=0.1$. Combining the mismatch with the signal-to-noise-dependent distinguishability criterion, we find that for a source at $1\,\mathrm{Gpc}$, eccentricities as small as $e_0\simeq5\times10^{-4}$ (B-DECIGO and IndIGO-D) and $5\times10^{-3}$ (LGWA-Si) are resolvable, one to two orders of magnitude below where detection efficiency degrades. The loss of match arises from the excitation of radial Teukolsky harmonics, which spread power into sidebands that circular templates cannot represent, and from the slower orbital decay of eccentric binaries at fixed initial semi-latus rectum. Robust detection and parameter estimation of IMRIs with DECIGO, LGWA, and IndIGO-D will require eccentric waveform models and template banks.

## 53. [The imprint of neutron escape on non-thermal protons in AGN coronae](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21047) <a id="link53"></a>

**Authors:** Antonio Ambrosone, Damiano F. G. Fiorillo

**Abstract:** The coronae of active galactic nuclei (AGN) are promising sites of proton acceleration to hundreds of TeV, potentially accounting for the neutrino excess from NGC 1068 reported by IceCube. We show that photohadronic proton-to-neutron conversion provides an important proton escape channel, as neutrons can leave the corona well before decaying. In magnetized turbulent coronae, neutron escape can reduce the characteristic neutrino energy by up to a factor of four and the integrated neutrino luminosity by approximately 30% at a fixed non-thermal proton energy density. More strikingly, proton-to-neutron conversion offers an escape route roughly two orders of magnitude faster than proton diffusion, requiring a correspondingly larger proton injection rate to sustain the non-thermal population. The effect is considerably weaker when proton escape is already ballistic, as expected in magnetic-reconnection scenarios. Therefore, neutron escape is essential for quantitatively connecting neutrino emission from AGN coronae to the efficiency of proton injection into the acceleration process.

## 54. [Faster $\chi^2$ periodogram: Accelerating linear algebra with fast Toeplitz solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20300) <a id="link54"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20300

**Authors:** Kacper Rutkowski, Rados{ł}aw Poleski

**Abstract:** In recent years, the traditional Lomb--Scargle periodogram, relying on least-squares fitting of a sinusoid to data with a known mean, has been mostly superseded by its floating-mean extension---the generalized Lomb--Scargle periodogram---due to the superior robustness and accuracy of the latter. While further generalization to higher-degree trigonometric polynomials (known as the multiharmonic analysis of variance and the fast $\chi^2$ periodogram) has been known for decades, its usage remains relatively uncommon, mostly due to its significantly higher computational cost. Here we present a technique reducing the computational complexity of such an extension by combining a Toeplitz formulation of the system of normal equations with non-uniform fast Fourier transforms, coupled with condition-number monitoring to protect against numerical instability. When modified for practical efficiency rather than asymptotic computational complexity, our approach reaches speed-ups exceeding an order of magnitude over current state-of-the-art implementations across a wide range of hyperparameters when evaluated on multithreaded systems, while matching or exceeding their accuracy. Additionally, we provide a reference implementation used for benchmarking, along with the code used to generate the results presented in this work.

## 55. [Towards Inter-satellite Laser Metrology for Formation Flying Interferometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20520) <a id="link55"></a>

**Authors:** A. O. Chan (ANU Center for Gravitational Astrophysics, ANU Research School of Astronomy & Astrophysics), J. E. Debs (ANU Department of Quantum Science and Technology), M. J. Ireland (ANU Research School of Astronomy & Astrophysics), C. P. Bandutunga (ANU Center for Gravitational Astrophysics)

**Abstract:** Context. The direct detection and atmospheric characterization of terrestrial exoplanets has been highlighted as a key science focus by the US ASTRO2020 Decadal survey and the ESA Voyage 2050 plan. This will require a new generation of telescope which is able to meet the contrast requirements necessary to image a planet many orders of magnitude fainter than its host star - a technological feat which currently limits our observation capabilities. In the mid-infrared, the most viable solution has been shown to be a formation-flying nulling space interferometer. Aims. In this work, we present the initial prototype for an inter-spacecraft metrology system. This system is a sub-system of the Pyxis/SEIRIOS interferometer, a formation-flying interferometer which serves as a technology demonstrator for the LIFE mission. Methods. We have designed a multi-stage metrology system using three interferometric measurement techniques, covering varying distance resolution ranges. Digital Interferometric (DI) ranging provides the coarsest measurement, while a differential phase read-out provides sub-nm precision. Multi-wavelength interferometry is employed to overcome the phase ambiguity associated with the differential phase readout. Results. A single-wavelength electronic noise floor below 20 pm RMS over 10 s was measured by applying a common photodetector signal to both readout channels, a factor of 28 below the 560 pm requirement. For the multi-wavelength readout, the obtained electronic noise RMS was 556 nm at 10 second integration, decreasing to 200 nm below 3 second integration. The full optical measurement in laboratory air is currently limited by environmental path fluctuations. Conclusions. We demonstrate an initial prototype system for precision ranging between satellites for formation-flying nulling interferometers.

**Abstract:** A new epoch for direct visualisation of accretion dynamics near a black hole's event horizon began when the Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration (EHTC) synthesised an Earth-sized aperture with millimeter radio interferometry and published the first reconstructed image of a black hole shadow at the core of radio galaxy Messier 87 (M87). The image appeared as a ring-like structure, representing the gravitationally lensed and redshifted emission around the central supermassive black hole, M87*, located at the galactic nucleus. However, application of various data analysis and imaging heuristics resulted in divergent representations of M87* fine-scale features, with some external analyses even challenging the fundamental ring-like morphology of the source. Such difficulties arise as a consequence of the inherent sparsity of the very-long baseline interferometry (VLBI) measurements, rendering interferometric image reconstruction an inadequately constrained problem. Moreover, the solution is sensitive to instrument calibration, post-processing algorithms, and imaging hyperparameters. Here, we apply a newly-developed independent imaging algorithm to the M87* dataset, which is free from prescriptive morphological priors and founded on observables immune to instrument calibration errors. We reconstruct the black hole shadow image, and unequivocally reproduce a ring-like morphology, with angular diameter 42.2 +/- 1.5 microarcseconds corresponding to a black hole mass of 6.5 +/- 0.5 billion solar masses assuming nominal scaling parameters, observed at an inclination angle of ~20 deg from the approaching jet.

Alternative scenarios include a jet-in-the-jet or a gamma-ray burst occurring within the AGN disk. However, the physical association between the transient and the AGN is not firmly established; for the radio-loud and X-ray-detected AGN counterpart class, the trial-corrected chance probability is $4.9 \times 10^{-3}$. **Abstract:** The indirect evidence for magnetic fields in the intergalactic medium motivates the study of their possible primordial origin, including generation during inflation. In this work, we study the viability of this mechanism in the context of low scale inflation, particularly on inflationary energy scales near the electroweak (EW) scale. We find that the post inflationary amplification of the gauge fields can produce astrophysically relevant magnetic fields for $g_{\phi\gamma}\ge 360~m_{\rm pl}^{-1}$ in the absence of Schwinger backreaction for an inflationary scale around the EW scale. We then include the backreaction of the charged current generated through the Schwinger mechanism. We find that the induced current suppresses the gauge field amplification and leads to a saturation of the gauge field energy density at a value of order $m_e^4$, where $m_e$ is the electron mass. Assuming this saturation level, we find that magnetic fields above the lower bound inferred from the non detection of secondary GeV photons from blazars can be obtained when reheating occurs at temperatures below $\sim 0.3$ GeV. For an inflationary energy scale of $100~{\rm GeV}$, achieving this saturation until a reheating temperature, $T\le 0.3~{\rm GeV}$, requires a coupling strength $g_{\phi\gamma}\ge 20000~m_{\rm pl}^{-1}$. We obtain a present-day magnetic field strength of $B_0 \sim 2\times10^{-13}$ G with a coherence length of $L_{0}\sim 5\times10^{-3}$ kpc for the reheating temperature of $100$ MeV.

**Authors:** Yashmitha Kumaran, Ilídio Lopes

**Abstract:** The Starobinsky scalaron is sourced by the trace of the stress--energy, so its exterior charge is set by the matter around an object and not by the object's vacuum geometry. We derive a master formula for that charge for any static, spherically symmetric source, evaluate it first for neutron stars, where the interior is known, and then for accreting black holes, where it is not. For a star the pressure subtracts: on the interior metric with the Eardley sensitivity, and the charge is a quasi-universal function of the compactness. Six realistic equations of state confirm the relation but lie above the coefficient calibrated on polytropes; the refit reproduces their mean to 0.007. The trace $\rho-3p$ changes sign in the cores of massive stars, and the charge also tracks the tidal deformability and the moment of inertia to a few parts in 1000 and 100. Solving the Einstein-frame structure equations self-consistently moves a star along this relation and leaves its charge at fixed compactness within 4 per cent of the test-field value. We compare the charge with NICER radii and the GW170817 tidal deformability, and use the published massless dipole constraint from GW230529 to calibrate a Fisher estimate of the sensitivity of a similar binary to a finite scalaron range, projected to the Einstein Telescope and Cosmic Explorer; the range is a free parameter, and the laboratory bound on pure $R+\alpha R^2$ is confronted explicitly. A vacuum black hole has q=0; a hole with a disc has $q=\sinh(m_\phi r_d)/(m_\phi r_d)$, controlled by the disc's outer edge, which makes it the counterpart in which the same integral is dominated by matter far from the centre; light is blind to the charge at leading order, and the correction beyond it is negligible for any self-consistent disc.

## 61. [Kepler as an accidental heliospheric imager: Thomson-scattered solar-wind structure in four years of background photometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19657) <a id="link61"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19657

**Authors:** Peter M. Shearer

**Abstract:** Kepler recorded several thousand dedicated sky-background pixels per detector output every 29.4 min from 2009 May to 2013 May while staring at a fixed 12-degree field at ecliptic latitude +65 degrees. From this archive we isolate a large-scale layer that measures brightness variations relative to an 8-day running median. This layer contains fluctuations of 0.02-0.1 electrons per second per pixel (about 1e-17 of the mean solar surface brightness) that sweep across the field in a day or less. Four lines of evidence show that these are sunlight Thomson-scattered by solar-wind electrons. (1) The sweeps propagate away from the Sun (84 percent within 45 degrees of the anti-solar direction; 25 percent expected by chance), and their direction follows the Sun through each quarter and across all 16 spacecraft rolls. (2) The field brightness correlates with the near-Earth (OMNI) proton density within a day of zero lag (r = 0.19, p < 1/150), but not with the energetic-proton flux. (3) Time-elongation maps show fronts moving outward at a median 15 degrees per day, as forward models of radially moving density structures predict. (4) The amplitude is about one third of a parameter-free Thomson-scattering prediction and varies by a factor of about 3 over the year, as predicted. Half of the scattered light comes from within 0.4-0.7 AU of the spacecraft. Fronts are strongest in slow wind. Kepler's background pixels thus provide a four-year, high-cadence, out-of-ecliptic record of solar-wind structure near 1 AU. Standard Kepler processing removes these signals, changing stellar light curves by less than about 1 ppm.

## 62. [Bayesian Time-Varying Spectral Estimation for LISA with P-Splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19293) <a id="link62"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19293

**Authors:** Avi Vajpeyi, Giorgio Mentasti, Ollie Burke, Renate Meyer, Patricio Maturana-Russel, Quentin Baghi

**Abstract:** We model the evolutionary spectrum of a slowly time-varying process by a linear combination of products of time- and frequency-dependent B-splines, placing a Gaussian tensor-product penalty prior on the coefficients. The posterior distribution is obtained by updating the prior using a Whittle-type likelihood approximation of the Wilson-Daubechies-Meyer time-frequency coefficients. Separate regularisation in time and frequency allows non-stationary spectral structure to be inferred. An adaptive coarse-grained likelihood reduces the computational cost while preserving fine resolution around sharp spectral features. We first validate the method using repeated simulations of a locally stationary process with known spectrum, showing lower reconstruction errors and narrower posterior intervals as the sample size increases. We then apply it to a year-long simulated LISA data set with stationary instrumental single link spectra, an evolving time-delay interferometry (TDI) response, and a modulated Galactic foreground. We compare a flexible total power spectral density (PSD) model with models incorporating progressively stronger physical information and examine both continuous and gapped observations. Allowing time variation substantially improves reconstruction relative to a stationary model, while incorporating physical information about the TDI response further improves recovery, particularly near its moving spectral features. The results illustrate the trade-off between spectral flexibility, physical assumptions, and posterior uncertainty in non-stationary LISA noise estimation.

## 63. [Repeated Gravitational-Wave Lensing from Binary Black Holes on Eccentric and Precessing Orbits around Spinning Supermassive Black Holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20416) <a id="link63"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20416

**Authors:** Jie Wu, Mengfei Sun, Jin Li, Zhoujian Cao

**Abstract:** Gravitational waves (GWs) from a stellar-mass binary black hole (BBH) orbiting a supermassive black hole (SMBH) can experience repeated gravitational lensing as the BBH passes behind the SMBH. Our previous studies of orbit-induced GW modulation motivate this investigation of repeated lensing in evolving hierarchical triple systems. We investigate how eccentricity, inclination, and relativistic precession of the outer orbit affect successive lensing events and parameter inference. The outer orbit follows post-Newtonian dynamics, and the inspiral waveform includes moving-source effects and de Sitter precession. An event-local point-mass lensing model tracks the changing source--lens alignment and GW frequency. For a one-year LISA observation, we examine lensing-event identification, Fisher-matrix estimates of parameter uncertainties, and waveform residuals when lensing is omitted. Our results show that outer-orbit evolution changes the timing and amplification of successive lensing events and can bring initially unfavorable configurations into the lensing region. A fixed circular, nonprecessing approximation misses all lensing events for a substantial fraction of the selected lensed systems. The median fractional reductions in uncertainty are largest for the outer-orbit inclination and SMBH spin. For some systems above the adopted detection threshold, lensing-induced waveform differences persist after local adjustments to the parameters of the unlensed model. Repeated lensing can thus provide complementary constraints on SMBH properties and outer-orbit geometry in LISA observations of hierarchical BBHs.

## 64. [Stripped White Dwarfs as Spectroscopic Clocks: Abundance Transitions in Partial Tidal Disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19867) <a id="link64"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19867

**Authors:** Taylor Yi-Ching Chong, Jin-Hong Chen, Lixin Dai

**Abstract:** We investigate partial tidal disruptions of He and CO white dwarfs (WDs) by a $10^{4}\,M_{\odot}$ intermediate-mass black hole (IMBH) using three-dimensional hydrodynamic simulations. We construct realistic WD structures and abundance profiles with MESA and map them into FLASH to follow the remnant response and the time-dependent composition of the fallback debris in single pericentre passages on tightly bound orbits ($e=0.9$). The element-specific fallback rates exhibit pre-peak compositional transitions that trace the WD's stratified layers. In the grazing encounter ($\beta=0.55$), hydrogen dominates the earliest returning debris, followed by helium, showing a H$\rightarrow$He transition. Deeper encounters ($\beta=0.7$ and $0.8$) penetrate the CO core and produce a He$\rightarrow$C transition, that occurs earlier relative to peak fallback and is stronger in the deeper encounter. These signatures provide a potential spectroscopic diagnostic for identifying CO-WD tidal disruption events (TDEs). We then develop a semi-analytic model for repeated tidal stripping and predict passage-to-passage compositional evolution, in which the stripped material becomes progressively enriched in heavier elements as deeper layers are exposed, leading to stronger and earlier abundance transitions in later flares. We find that WDs can survive several dozen ($\sim 30$-$80$) passages, depending on their initial mass and compactness. Our results link time-dependent abundance signatures to repeating WD disruptions and provide testable observable predictions for identifying IMBH-powered transients.

## 65. [The rise and fall of 1A 1118-61: First evidence for frequency hysteresis of quasi-periodic oscillations in an X-ray pulsar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20091) <a id="link65"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20091

**Authors:** Alexander Salganik, Sergey S. Tsygankov, Sergey V. Molkov, Hua Xiao, Lingda Kong, QingChang Zhao, Long Ji, Alexander A. Mushtukov, Igor Yu. Lapshov, Alexander A. Lutovinov, Alexey Yu. Tkachenko, Hua Feng, Shuang-Nan Zhang, Xiao-Bo Li, Shu Zhang, Juri Poutanen

**Abstract:** Context. Quasi-periodic oscillations (QPOs) are commonly observed in accreting X-ray pulsars (XRPs), but the physical mechanisms governing their frequency evolution remain poorly understood.   Aims. We investigate the evolution of the QPO during the 2026 outburst of the Be/X-ray pulsar 1A 1118-61, focusing on its frequency-luminosity relation during the rising and declining phases of the outburst.   Methods. We analyzed X-ray timing and spectral observations obtained with SRG/ART-XC, NuSTAR, and Insight-HXMT. We measured QPO centroid frequencies and amplitudes, compared their evolution during the rising and declining phases of the outburst, and examined the associated changes in the X-ray spectral shape.   Results. We detected a prominent QPO with a centroid frequency ranging from approximately 37 to 114 mHz. Its frequency-luminosity relation follows two distinct branches, with QPO frequencies approximately 35% higher during the rise than during the decline at the same luminosity. The frequency evolution along both branches is consistent with Keplerian motion near the inner disk edge. The X-ray spectral shape also follows different evolutionary tracks during the rising and declining phases.   Conclusions. We report the first evidence for QPO frequency hysteresis in an XRP. The observed separation between the rising and declining branches suggests that the characteristic timescale of the QPO depends not only on the instantaneous luminosity but also on the accretion history. We discuss possible implications for the disk-magnetosphere interaction.

## 66. [Maximum entropy reconstruction of the black hole greybody factors from its Korteweg-de Vries integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21043) <a id="link66"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21043

**Authors:** Noah Alberca (Departament de Matemàtiques, Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona, Institute of Space Sciences), Michele Lenzi (Physique Théorique et Mathématique, Université Libre de Bruxelles), Carlos F. Sopuerta (Institute of Space Sciences, Institute of Space Studies of Catalonia)

**Abstract:** The dynamics of perturbed non-rotating black holes (BHs) can be described by master wave equations with a potential barrier. Recent work has shown that these equations possess an infinite number of symmetries generated by the Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) hierarchy, leading to conserved quantities (KdV integrals) that fully determine the BH greybody factors via a moment problem. In this paper, we introduce and validate a new method to solve this moment problem using the maximum entropy principle. The method reconstructs, at each order of truncation, the least-biased transmission probability compatible with a finite set of KdV integrals, guaranteeing positivity by construction. We first validate the technique against the exactly solvable P\"oschl-Teller potential. We then apply it to the Regge-Wheeler potential governing axial perturbations of a Schwarzschild BH, where no exact solution is available; the resulting reconstruction agrees closely with the fourth-order Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin (WKB) approximation across several multipoles. This is the first application of the maximum entropy method to the reconstruction of BH greybody factors, demonstrating that a modest number of KdV integrals is sufficient to recover them accurately.

Ravi, David J. Sand, Manisha Shrestha, Bhagya M. Subrayan, Moira Andrews, Joseph R. Farah, D. Andrew Howell, Curtis McCully, Megan Newsome, Yuan Qi Ni, Estefania Padilla-Gonzalez, Giacomo Terreran, Kathryn Wynn, Joshua B. Haislip, Vladimir V. Kouprianov, Daniel E. Reichart

**Abstract:** We present the high-cadence light curve and rapid spectroscopy of the Type II SN 2024phv, discovered just 16.8 hours after the estimated explosion time. The early light curve exhibits a rapid 2 mag rise over the first day of observation, and spectra at 1.7 and 2.6 days reveal weak, narrow emission lines, suggesting moderate interaction with circumstellar material during the first few days after explosion. Models of shock-cooling emission provide a reasonable fit to the light curve after day 2, implying minimal interaction at later epochs, and yield a best-fit progenitor radius of $R=390\pm90\,R_{\odot}$. We also compare our earliest spectra with radiation hydrodynamical models of exploding red supergiants to measure circumstellar densities of ${\sim}10^{-12}$ g cm$^{-3}$ within a few stellar radii of the progenitor, followed by a sharp dropoff in the density profile. This rapidly decreasing profile leads us to conclude that the progenitor of SN 2024phv had an extended atmosphere and/or closely confined circumstellar material, likely from rapidly increasing or eruptive mass loss prior to explosion. In the context of other supernovae discovered at similarly early phases, SN 2024phv points to a diversity in the mass-loss rates of red supergiant stars in their final years.

## 68. [Unveiling Hidden X-ray Broad Line Region in Centaurus A: Broadband Analysis with XRISM and NuSTAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19327) <a id="link68"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19327

**Authors:** Yuya Nakatani, Yoshihiro Ueda, David Bogensberger, Taishu Kayanoki, Yasushi Fukazawa, Luigi Gallo, Richard Mushotzky, Tahir Yaqoob, Kotaro Fukushima, Bert Vander Meulen, Junjie Mao, Kanta Fujiwara, Kouichi Hagino, Toshiya Iwata, Jon M. Miller, Misaki Mizumoto, Hirofumi Noda, Makoto Tashiro, Misaki Urata

**Abstract:** We present a broadband X-ray spectral analysis of the radio galaxy Centaurus~A, utilizing simultaneous \textit{XRISM} (Resolve and Xtend) and \textit{NuSTAR} observations. Unlike the previous phenomenological analysis of the Fe K$\alpha$ line (Bogensberger et al. 2025), we employ physical reflection models developed with the radiative transfer code SKIRT. They self-consistently predict the continuum and fluorescent lines from a clumpy torus and an X-ray broad line region (xBLR). The Fe K$\alpha$ complex is well reproduced with a three velocity component model. We interpret this as two narrow torus components (FWHM $= 310\pm50$ and $1900\pm400$~km~s$^{-1}$) and an xBLR component (FWHM $= 6000_{-800}^{+1100}$~km~s$^{-1}$). The inner torus radius of 0.13~pc is 10 times the dust sublimation radius (0.013~pc), implying that the torus in a low-luminosity AGN is not determined by dust-related physics. The xBLR has a substantial covering factor of 0.11--0.36, a hydrogen column density ($5 \times 10^{23}$~cm$^{-2}$), a radius of 0.014~pc from the SMBH, and a mass of $>0.9 M_\odot$. Its mass supply rate is comparable to the accretion rate inferred from the bolometric luminosity, implying efficient xBLR formation even at low Eddington ratios. The xBLR line width also provides a novel estimate of the black-hole mass, $\log{(M_{\rm BH}/M_{\odot})} = 7.00\pm0.34$, via the optical BLR mass-scaling relation to the Fe~K$\alpha$. This agrees with dynamical measurements within a factor of $\sim5$, consistent with the scatter of the optical relation. We also infer the abundance ratios of $A_{\rm Ca}/A_{\rm Fe} = 1.1_{-0.1}^{+0.2}$ and $A_{\rm Ni}/A_{\rm Fe} = 1.4_{-0.2}^{+0.1}$ solar.

## 69. [Testing General Relativity by Reconstructing Dark Energy and the Growth Index with Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19117) <a id="link69"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19117

**Authors:** Andronikos Paliathanasis

**Abstract:** We extend the Cosmo-PINN, a physics-informed neural network for model-independent cosmological reconstruction, to reconstruct the dark energy equation of state parameter $w_{\mathrm{DE}}(z)$ and the growth index $\gamma (z)$ of matter perturbations. The reconstruction is performed through three modules: the background module, the matter growth module, and the growth index module. The background module learns the dark energy density $\rho _{\mathrm{DE}}(z)$ from cosmological observations, and $w_{\mathrm{DE}}(z)$ is reconstructed through the residual of the conservation equation. The reconstructed background is then passed to the other two modules, which reconstruct the observables $f(z)$ and $f\sigma _{8}(z)$ either directly from the linear perturbation equation of General Relativity or through an independently reconstructed growth index $\gamma (z)$. Comparing the two reconstructions on the same background provides a consistency test of the underlying physical framework. The network is trained on the DESI DR2 Baryon Acoustic Oscillations, Cosmic Chronometers, Type Ia Supernovae, and redshift-space distortion data. Posterior uncertainties for the reconstructed cosmological parameters and functions are obtained through Hamiltonian Monte Carlo sampling. For the different data combinations, the posterior median of the present-day dark energy equation of state lies within the range $w_{\mathrm{DE,0}}\in \lbrack -0.930,-0.867]$, while the phantom divide crossing takes place at $z\simeq 0.41$--$0.53$. The posterior median of the present-day growth index reconstructed from the data lies within $\gamma _{0}\simeq 0.60$--$0.62$, indicating suppression of the growth of cosmic structure.

## 70. [Hunting dark matter: the present and future of direct particle detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21052) <a id="link70"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21052

**Authors:** Felix Kahlhoefer, Belina von Krosigk, Teresa Marrodán Undagoitia, Valentina Novati, María Luisa Sarsa

**Abstract:** The nature of dark matter remains one of the major open questions in particle physics and cosmology. Direct detection experiments aim to identify dark matter particles through their interactions with ordinary matter, but despite substantial improvements in experimental sensitivity, no conclusive detection has been achieved. In this review, we provide a comprehensive overview of the present status and future prospects of direct particle detection, focusing on searches for dark matter particles from the Galactic halo. We introduce the theoretical framework for interpreting experimental results, including nuclear and electron scattering, collective excitations, particle absorption, and time- and direction-dependent signatures. We review the detection technologies employed in these searches, highlighting their operating principles, advantages, and limitations, as well as the calibration techniques and background mitigation strategies required to achieve high sensitivity. Particular emphasis is placed on experimental results reported since 2020, covering nuclear and electron recoil searches, annual modulation measurements, and directional detection. We also discuss experiments under construction and proposed future detectors, ranging from large-scale noble-liquid experiments to low-threshold technologies targeting sub-GeV dark matter. Finally, we examine the challenges that will shape the next generation of experiments, including the need for increasingly low energy thresholds, improved background understanding and discrimination, and the growing importance of neutrino-induced backgrounds. The diversity and complementarity of detector technologies will remain essential for exploring the dark matter parameter space and establishing the nature of any potential signal.

## 71. [A hybrid log-likelihood-ratio statistic for single-detector SPIIR gravitational-wave searches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19918) <a id="link71"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19918

**Authors:** SPIIR Collaboration, Weichangfeng Guo, Sunil Choudhary, Victor Oloworaran, Linqing Wen, Jayden Doan, Qingyuan Liang, Tim Davies, Luke Davis, Irina Dvorkin, Apratim Ganguly, Shang-Jie Jin, JeongCho Kim, Yu-Chiung Lin, Ngai-Chun Lung, Alistair McLeod, Khun Sang Phukon, Andreas Wicenec

**Abstract:** Periods with only one detector operating accounted for 13.3 percent of the third LIGO-Virgo observing run (O3) and 29.7 percent of the first part of the fourth observing run (O4a). We present the first dedicated single-detector implementation of the Summed Parallel Infinite Impulse Response (SPIIR) pipeline for binary neutron star (BNS) and neutron star-black hole (NSBH) merger searches. Our hybrid log-likelihood ratio combines signal-consistency models for non-Gaussian glitches with a profile likelihood ratio for the signal-to-noise ratio under a Gaussian-noise model. We calibrate the glitch model by source category and trigger-rate class. We assign false alarm rates (FARs) using the measured background from the same detector and analysis chunk, extrapolating its high-ranking tail. We check the FAR assignments by comparing observed and expected cumulative background counts for BNS in O3a and NSBH in O3b at LIGO Hanford (H1) and LIGO Livingston (L1). Using O3 data and the GWTC-3 injection set, we find that, at a threshold of one false alarm per two months, the BNS sensitive distances are $136.5^{+2.9}_{-3.0}$ Mpc in L1 and $120.2^{+3.1}_{-3.3}$ Mpc in H1. These central estimates are approximately 24 and 38 percent higher, respectively, than the GWTC-3 GstLAL values of $110.5^{+3.2}_{-3.4}$ Mpc and $87.2^{+3.6}_{-3.9}$ Mpc over the same data and injection parameter ranges. GstLAL provides the only published single-detector results in GWTC-3. For NSBH, SPIIR reaches $164.1^{+6.7}_{-7.3}$ Mpc in L1 and $134.9^{+6.3}_{-7.0}$ Mpc in H1, compared with $165.1^{+6.7}_{-7.3}$ Mpc and $156.0^{+5.9}_{-6.4}$ Mpc for GWTC-3 GstLAL, respectively. Targeted searches recover triggers associated with GW190425, GW200105_162426, and GW230529_181500 with assigned FARs below the same threshold.

## 72. [Global Fit Inference for LISA with samsara++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17266) <a id="link72"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.17266

**Authors:** Lorenzo Valbusa Dall'Armi, Gabriele Astorino, Riccardo Buscicchio, Angelo Ricciardone, Walter Del Pozzo

**Abstract:** The Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA) will observe a superposition of gravitational-wave signals, requiring joint inference of an unknown number of sources, parameters, and instrumental noise. To disentangle these contributions, that overlap in both time and frequency, a Global Fit (GF) analysis is needed. We present samsara++, a C++/CUDA sampler designed to address this trans-dimensional GF problem. Building on the continuous-time MCMC framework of samsara, the algorithm combines birth-death processes for adding and removing sources, with mutation moves for parameter estimation. Birth and death are selected through posterior-dependent rates. Shared-memory parallelism, incremental likelihood updates, and indexed storage reduce the computational and memory costs, while parallel tempering improves the exploration of multimodal posteriors. We assess the performance using simulated one-year LISA datasets containing Galactic double white dwarf binaries and stationary Gaussian instrumental noise. Analyses of populations containing 143 and 866 binaries start from empty source models, without preliminary searches or proposals tailored to the injected population. These tests demonstrate source reconstruction and catalog generation, highlighting, at the same time, the importance of source-count, overlapping signals, and incomplete mixing on joint source and noise inference. Then we perform a separate stress test, initialized at the injected parameters, exploring a population of 16,435 binaries, corresponding to 131,480 source parameters. This run completes 15 million iterations in approximately 16 hours using about 3 GB of RAM. All together, these benchmarks show the computational feasibility of birth-death sampling for large Galactic-binary populations, and provide a foundation for extending samsara++ toward a comprehensive LISA global fit.

## 73. [Magnetic Power in Binaries: From Coalescing Binary Neutron Stars to Close-in Exoplanetary Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20654) <a id="link73"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20654

**Authors:** Dong Lai, Tu Guo, Nicholas Corso

**Abstract:** A binary system containing two magnetic components ("stars") can be modeled as a circuit powered by the orbital motion, with the magnetic field lines linking the two stars serving as wires. When the total resistance in the binary components is small -- as found in many astrophysical systems such as binary neutron stars and extra-solar hot-Jupiter or super-Earth systems -- the connected field lines are twisted and open up in a quasi-cyclic manner, leading to quasi-periodic energy dissipation and flares. We derive analytic expressions for the average and episodic magnetic powers from such systems, providing a quantitative description on how the powers depend on the magnetic orientations of the two stars. The power can be much larger than in the case where one of the stars has a negligible magnetic field. It may manifest as pre-merger quasi-cyclic flares in coalescing neutron-star binaries and as orbit-regulated radio emissions in exoplanetary systems.

**Abstract:** We present spectro-temporal review of $34$ black hole X-ray binaries (BH-XRBs), classified as persistent-cum-variable, persistent and transient, observed with \textit{AstroSat} during its first decade of operations. Among persistent sources, we report the first detections of a type-A LFQPO ($\sim2.7\text{ Hz}$) in 4U 1957$+$11 and type-B LFQPOs ($4-8\text{ Hz}$) in 4U 1630$-$472. Broadband thermal disc along with Comptonization modelling reveals tight correlations among QPO frequency, time-lag, rms variability, photon index ($\Gamma_{\rm th}$), disc-to-Comptonized flux ratio ($F_{\rm ratio}$), and bolometric luminosity ($L_{\rm bol}$), tracing disc-corona-jet coupling and identifying `canonical' spectral states (LHS, HIMS, SIMS, HSS). Type-C QPOs exhibit soft-lags with occasional reversals at high coronal fractions ($>0.8$), whereas type-B QPOs show hard-lags at lower fractions ($<0.5$), linking to disc-jet coupling. Supported by simulations and radiation driven spectral modelling, these results favor a unified accretion framework while outlining limitations, challenges and future prospects.

## 75. [Conditions for mass-loading: Investigating jet-disk material exchange in strongly magnetised accretion flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21401) <a id="link75"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21401

**Authors:** Subhrat Praharaj, M. T. P. Liska, Sera B. Markoff, Koushik Chatterjee, Bart Ripperda, Lorenzo Sironi, and, Aylecia S. Lattimer

**Abstract:** Relativistic jets are produced by accreting black holes across a wide range of masses, from stellar-mass X-ray binaries to supermassive active galactic nuclei (AGN). In low-luminosity AGN, these jets are believed to be powered by strongly magnetised accretion flows, a picture supported by Event Horizon Telescope and VLBI observations of M87. The jets often exhibit high-energy flaring emission which, if partly hadronic, implies that baryons must be loaded into the jet and accelerated despite strong magnetic barriers. However, the mechanism behind this remains unclear. We present high-resolution, three-dimensional General Relativistic Magnetohydrodynamic (GRMHD) simulations of a high-spin Magnetically Arrested Disk (MAD), a strongly magnetised accretion regime. Using passive Eulerian tracers, we track the exchange of material between the jet and disk to quantify mixing. We find that magnetic flux eruptions, a hallmark of MAD accretion, expel magnetic flux tubes initially filled with jet material (expected to be pair-dominated in real systems). Through magnetic reconnection during these eruptions, this material is mixed into the disk. Crucially, the same flux tubes reconnect the disk back to the jet, allowing disk material, expected to be primarily hadronic, to infiltrate the jet sheath and boundary. This disk-to-jet injection offers a natural mass-loading mechanism that does not require the jet to be globally baryon-dominated. The resulting ejection-injection cycle may instead drive episodic hadronic acceleration, providing a viable pathway for high-energy emission in relativistic jets.

## 76. [Simultaneous tests of post-Newtonian inspiral using GW250114 and GW250207](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20738) <a id="link76"></a>

**Authors:** Saurabh Kumar, Parthapratim Mahapatra, Ish Mohan Gupta, M. Saleem, B. S. Sathyaprakash, K. G. Arun

**Abstract:** We perform the one of the most general tests of post-Newtonian (PN) inspiral in binary black holes to date by allowing all eight phase coefficients up to 3.5PN order to vary simultaneously from their general-relativistic values. Using two of the loudest binary black hole events observed to date, GW250114 and GW250207, we carry out Bayesian inference with the IMRPhenomXPHM_SpinTaylor waveform model augmented by parametrized deviations in the post-Newtonian phasing. We then identify the combinations of phase coefficients most strongly constrained by the data through a principal component analysis of the eight-dimensional deformation space. We find that the data constrain the three leading principal components beyond their priors, and that all three are consistent with the predictions of general relativity. These results demonstrate that exceptionally loud gravitational-wave events enable robust, high-dimensional tests of inspiral dynamics and provide direct access to the measurable subspace of post-Newtonian deformations.

**Authors:** A. Haris, N. Routamo, T. Willamo, H. Hilo, A. Luttinen, J. J. Lehtinen, O. Kochukhov, T. Hackman, I. Ilyin, M. J. Korpi-Lagg, S. Wedemeyer, M. Tuomi

**Abstract:** Studying the magnetic activity of young solar analogues is important for understanding the dynamo mechanisms of the Sun and other late-type stars. We aimed to determine the surface magnetic field topology of four stars, analogues to the young Sun. This will contribute to long-term studies of the evolution of magnetic activity on different timescales of these targets. We obtained high resolution spectropolarimetric measurements with the HARPSpol instrument mounted at the ESO 3.6m telescope in the epoch 2021.6, and the SOFIN instrument, mounted on the 2.56m NOT telescope in epochs 2017.4 and 2021.5. The signal-to-noise ratio of the observations was boosted with least squares deconvolution. The surface magnetic field maps were calculated with the Zeeman-Doppler imaging technique. The magnetic field is decomposed into spherical harmonic expansions, allowing us to quantify the contributions from the axisymmetric and non-axisymmetric components, as well as the poloidal and toroidal fields. Out of the four stars observed (BE Cet, HN Peg, kappa1 Cet, V1358 Ori), three (BE Cet, HN Peg, kappa1 Cet) show clear signs of magnetic fields. Only one star, BE Cet shows clear evidence for the dominance of non-axisymmetric modes and the poloidal field being more dominant than the toroidal. HN Peg also shows signs of a stronger poloidal than a toroidal field, indicating an {\alpha}^2 dynamo, where the convective turbulence effects dominate over the differential rotation. The magnetic field of V1358 Ori could not be reliably reconstructed.

## 78. [Geometric tracking of a halo CME using full-circle wide-field views from PUNCH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20290) <a id="link78"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20290

**Authors:** Craig E. DeForest, Kieran A. Russell, Samuel Van Kooten, Heather A. Elliott, Maher A. Dayeh, Sarah E. Gibson, Anna Malanushenko, David F. Webb, Ronnie Killough, Jillian Redfern, William L. Wells, Matthew N. Beasley, Glenn T. Laurent, Nick R. Waltham, J. Marcus Hughes, Daniel B. Seaton, Ritesh Patel, Chris Lowder, Derek A. Lamb, Alec R. DeForest

**Abstract:** We tracked the large, geoeffective halo coronal mass ejection (CME) of 2025-May-31 across the very wide field of view of the Polarimeter to Unify the Corona and Heliosphere (PUNCH) mission, as a proof of concept for data-informed forecasting of CME arrival times at Earth. using simple point-and-click measurement of the front location as far as 47 degrees away from the Sun itself, we constrained an analytic "ludicrous ice-cream cone" geometric model with just three parameters: position angle, off-Earth-Sun angle, and propagation speed. We show that the model is sufficient to capture the bulk envelope of the CME, and also insufficient to capture the complexity of the CME's evolution. We generated simulated arrival-time forecasts for the CME by extrapolating the constant-velocity propagation to determine the time of impact with Earth. These "retro-forecasts" converged to within 30 +/- 15 minutes of the actual arrival time, 8 hours prior to arrival. The convergence was marked by a clear inflection point in the curve of predicted-arrival-time vs time-of-forecast. We conclude that continuous wide-field tracking is sufficiently powerful to enable even crude models to substantially improve arrival-time forecasting precision, and discuss future work required both to validate this single-event result and to extend it to more sophisticated analyses.

## 79. [Neural Surrogates for Tabulated Nuclear Equations of State in Core-Collapse Supernova Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19400) <a id="link79"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19400

**Authors:** Youhei Masada, Jin Matsumoto, Tomoya Takiwaki

**Abstract:** Large-scale core-collapse supernova simulations rely on tabulated nuclear equations of state (EOSs), whose memory requirements can become a practical constraint in massively parallel calculations. We explore a compact alternative by replacing the tabulated thermodynamic mapping with a neural-network surrogate, using the Lattimer--Swesty EOS as a representative reference model. The surrogate directly predicts pressure, sound speed, and effective adiabatic index from $(\log \rho, \log e, Y_e)$ and accurately reconstructs the nonlinear thermodynamic structure of the EOS, including its behavior across phase-transition regions. It reproduces one-dimensional shock-tube solutions with hydrodynamic errors at the $\sim 10^{-3}$ level under different thermodynamic conditions and yields hydrostatic equilibrium configurations closely matching those obtained with the reference EOS. We find that smaller regression errors do not necessarily yield better hydrodynamic performance, demonstrating the importance of hydrodynamic validation in surrogate model selection. A compact network with only $\sim 10^4$ parameters retains this accuracy while reducing the memory required to store the EOS from $\sim 160$ MB to $\sim 100$ KB, corresponding to a reduction by a factor of $\sim 1500$. The surrogate also remains stable through collapse, bounce, shock formation, and post-bounce evolution in a one-dimensional adiabatic core-collapse calculation. These results demonstrate the potential of compact neural EOS surrogates for reducing memory requirements in large-scale core-collapse supernova simulations.

Lu, G. P. Lynch, T. J. Maccarone, A. S. Maniyar, E. S. Martsen, F. Menanteau, M. Millea, J. Montgomery, Y. Nakato, T. Natoli, A. Ouellette, Z. Pan, P. Paschos, K. A. Phadke, K. Prabhu, W. Quan, A. Rahlin, C. L. Reichardt, M. Rouble, J. E. Ruhl, A. C. Silva Oliveira, A. Simpson, J. A. Sobrin, A. A. Stark, J. Stephen, C. Tandoi, Q. Taylor, C. Trendafilova, J. D. Vieira, A. G. Vieregg, A. Vitrier, Y. Wan, N. Whitehorn, W. L. K. Wu, M. R. Young, J. A. Zebrowski

**Abstract:** We search for anisotropic cosmic birefringence using two years of data from the SPT-3G instrument on the South Pole Telescope. Anisotropic cosmic birefringence refers to a theorized dependence of the refractive index of the vacuum on the polarization of light that varies across the Universe in a statistically isotropic manner. Such an effect would manifest as a direction-dependent mixing of the $E$ and $B$ polarization modes of the cosmic microwave background (CMB), caused by rotation of the polarization angle as photons travel from the CMB last scattering surface to us. We present a measurement of the power spectrum of the rotation angle $\alpha$ in six bins across the angular multipole range $20 < L < 2048$, finding no evidence for cosmic birefringence with the spectrum consistent with zero. At 95% confidence, we set a new best upper limit on the amplitude of a scale-invariant birefringence power spectrum of $A_{\rm CB}<0.027$. Additionally, we place upper limits on a scale-dependent white noise model. We translate the scale-invariant upper limit to constraints on the axion coupling constant and primordial magnetic field strength, finding 95% confidence limits of $g_{\alpha\gamma} < 1.7\times 10^{-2}/H_I$ and $B_{\rm 1\,Mpc} < 9.6\,{\rm nG}$ respectively. We expect these limits to improve several-fold with the complete SPT-3G survey.

## 81. [The Effect of Asphericity to the Inference of Supernova Circumstellar Environment from Radio Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20672) <a id="link81"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20672

**Authors:** Ryotaro Chiba, Zhengwei Liu, Takashi J. Moriya, Xiaolong Yang

**Abstract:** Radio observations of supernovae (SNe) enable us to constrain the circumstellar environment of their progenitor stars, and hence the mass-loss history immediately prior to the explosion. However, despite lines of evidence pointing to the aspherical configurations of circumstellar matter (CSM) around many SNe, the derivation of CSM parameters is often done under the assumption of spherically symmetric CSM. In this work, based on a recent model of radio emission from SNe interacting with aspherical CSM, we investigate how lifting the assumption of spherical symmetry affects the CSM parameter inference. First, we apply the model to mock data simulating typical observations of Type Ia-CSM, Ibc and II SNe and find that the total CSM mass required for to reproduce radio observation depends weakly on the CSM covering fraction $f_\Omega$, with ${f_\Omega}^{0.15-0.38}$. We then apply the model and fit the radio light curves of Type Ia-CSM SN 2020eyj. We show that, even with the assumption of asphericity as severe as $f_\Omega = 0.1$, the requirement for the total CSM mass is only reduced to $\sim 50 \%$ of the value inferred in spherical models. These results demonstrate that introducing asphericity in radio emission models is often not enough to reduce the required CSM mass significantly.

**Abstract:** We need spectroscopic imaging at sub-millimetre wavelengths to answer fundamental questions about the origins of our universe, structure formation and galaxy evolution. Often, there are steep trade-offs between the number of detectors and their spectral resolving power in completing such 3D surveys. Novel astrophotonics technologies, such as the Superconducting On-Chip Fourier Transform Spectrometer (SOFTS), are underway to break instrumentation barriers and enable the packing of a focal-plane array with many high spectral resolution submillimeter detectors. Here, we summarize recent advances in SOFTS development, focusing on potential spectroscopic resolution and packing density within a focal plane array.

## 83. [Kinetic Monte Carlo modeling of ammonium formate NH$_4^+$HCOO$^-$ and ammonium cyanate NH$_4^+$OCN$^-$ in interstellar ices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21433) <a id="link83"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21433

**Authors:** Joseph Salaris, Robin Toonen, Rens Waters, Herma Cuppen

**Abstract:** JWST observations have revealed evidence for NH$_4^+$ in the ices of the Chamaeleon I dark molecular cloud, with HCOO$^-$ and OCN$^-$ likely counter ions. Laboratory experiments have demonstrated that ammonium formate and ammonium cyanate can form through acid-base reactions involving NH$_3$ at cryogenic temperatures. The formation of these two ammonium salts are simulated to investigate their formation in dark-cloud ices and assess their sensitivity to uncertainties in the chemical reaction network, including the recently considered HOCO-mediated pathway for CO$_2$ formation, as well as variations in the cosmic-ray ionization rate, cloud collapse timescale and density, and elemental depletion. Our modeling framework consists of microscopic kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) simulations of grain-surface chemistry augmented by UCLCHEM gas-phase and physical cloud evolution simulations. The microscopic KMC method provides for explicit simulation of the location, movement and reaction of ice species, making it particularly well-suited to model acid-base chemistry of NH$_4^+$-salt formation, occurring within the ice mantle over extended timescales. The simulations produce the canonical ice-evolution sequence, with an initially H$_2$O-rich ice followed by a layer dominated by CO and CO$_2$. Salt production is dominated by NH$_4^+$HCOO$^-$, which is found to form predominantly during the H$_2$O-rich stage as its production is limited by the availability of NH$_3$. NH$_4^+$OCN$^-$ is found to form later at the interface between the H$_2$O- and CO-rich ice when the formation of its precursor acid HNCO is enabled. Despite the variations explored, the total NH$_4^+$ abundance, particularly relative to H$_2$O, remains remarkably robust and consistent with the observed abundance. Depending on the variation, a redistribution between counterions HCOO$^-$ and OCN$^-$ is observed.

## 84. [Relativistic precession around rotating neutron stars. I. Slow rotation: Hartle--Thorne spacetimes, particle orbits and the QPOs of atoll and Z sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19502) <a id="link84"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19502

**Authors:** A. C. Khunt

**Abstract:** We study the relativistic precession model (RPM) of quasi-periodic oscillations (QPOs) in the spacetimes of slowly rotating neutron stars computed from the equation of state (EOS). For four EOSs (WFF1, G3, FSUGarnet and IOPB-I) we solve the Hartle--Thorne equations to second order in the angular velocity and compute the orbital and epicyclic frequencies of the exterior metric without expanding in the spin or the quadrupole. At $1.9\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$ the spin-induced quadrupole is 2--4 times that of a black hole with the same spin; it lowers the nodal precession frequency by up to 25 per cent near the innermost stable circular orbit and limits the nodal frequency that any spin can produce. An eccentric, tilted geodesic around a $1.92\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$ star spinning at 363~Hz precesses at the epicyclic rates, and the power spectrum of a hot spot on it peaks at the orbital, periastron and nodal precession frequencies. Applied to the atoll sources 4U 1636$-$53 and 4U 1728$-$34, the RPM gives masses of 2.02--$2.15\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$ and 1.88--$1.92\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$. For 4U 1728$-$34 the low-frequency QPO favours stars with large moments of inertia, $I\simeq2.5\times10^{45}$~g\,cm$^{2}$. Without an EOS the QPOs give $M=1.920^{+0.014}_{-0.012}\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$, $j=0.162^{+0.023}_{-0.011}$ and $\bar Q=2.2^{+1.1}_{-0.8}$; a Kerr spacetime with the same nodal frequency would underestimate the moment of inertia by 22 per cent. For the Z sources GX 5$-$1 and GX 340+0 the horizontal-branch oscillations cannot be twice the nodal precession frequency for any of the four EOSs: the fits are driven to spins of 730--745~Hz and to the maximum mass.

## 85. [Constraining neutrino masses with localized fast radio bursts: Current bounds and future prospects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19416) <a id="link85"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19416

**Authors:** Yin-Long Cao, Ji-Guo Zhang, Guo-Hong Du, Tian-Nuo Li, Jing-Fei Zhang, Xin Zhang

**Abstract:** The dispersion-measure (DM)--redshift relation of fast radio bursts (FRBs) provides independent low-redshift cosmological information for neutrino-mass inference. In this work, we perform joint analyses with cosmic microwave background (CMB) data, using 120 localized FRBs with reliable host-galaxy redshifts and larger mock samples, to investigate the effects of sample size and the dark-energy model on constraints on the sum of neutrino masses, $\sum m_\nu$. In the $\Lambda$CDM+$\sum m_\nu$ model, CMB+FRB gives $\sum m_\nu<0.21\,\mathrm{eV}$, substantially relaxed relative to the current CMB+DESI result. With 1000 simulated events, CMB+FRB1(mock) gives an upper limit of $0.12\,\mathrm{eV}$, which decreases to $0.035\,\mathrm{eV}$ for CMB+FRB2(mock) with 10000 events. The latter is more stringent than the CMB+DESI limit, demonstrating the potential of large localized FRB samples to constrain neutrino mass. When the model is extended to $w_0w_a$CDM+$\sum m_\nu$, both the observed and mock samples yield upper limits of approximately $0.3$--$0.4\,\mathrm{eV}$, indicating that the assumed dark-energy model remains a dominant factor in these constraints. As localized FRB samples grow and the modeling of the individual DM contributions becomes more accurate, FRBs may provide further independent low-redshift information for constraining neutrino mass.

## 86. [Gradients of Turbulent Fluctuations as New Tracers of Astrophysical Magnetic Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18980) <a id="link86"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18980

**Authors:** Yue Hu

**Abstract:** Magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence is ubiquitous in astrophysical plasmas and produces scale-dependent anisotropy with respect to the local magnetic field. Because turbulence cascades preferentially perpendicular to the field, its spatial gradients acquire preferred orientations that can be used to probe magnetic-field structure. This property underlies a family of gradient techniques based on spectroscopic, synchrotron-intensity, and synchrotron-polarization observations. In this review, we summarize the theoretical foundation of these techniques, including the anisotropy of MHD turbulence and the corresponding behavior of velocity, magnetic-field, and density fluctuations. We then describe how these fluctuations are encoded in spectroscopic data and synchrotron observables, and review the implementation of Velocity Centroid Gradients, Velocity Channel Gradients, Intensity Gradients, Synchrotron Intensity Gradients, and Synchrotron Polarization Gradients. We also discuss the effects of isotropic fast modes, shocks, self-gravity, stellar outflows, thermal broadening, and self-absorption. Finally, we review applications to the diffuse interstellar medium, star-forming regions, the Galactic center, external galaxies, and galaxy clusters.

## 87. [Magnetic fields in blazar jets derived from synchrotron self-absorption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19906) <a id="link87"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19906

**Authors:** Hubing Xiao, Junhang Liu, Shaopu Wang, Jianghe Yang, Zhihao Ouyang, Minfeng Gu, Shaohua Zhang, Zhijian Luo, Junhui Fan

**Abstract:** Context. Magnetic fields are believed to play a crucial role in the launching, acceleration, and evolution of blazar jets. However, direct measurements of the magnetic field strength in the radio-emitting regions of blazar jets remain limited. Aims. We aim to estimate the magnetic field strengths in the synchrotron self-absorption (SSA) regions of blazar jets and investigate their connections with the properties of the jet and central engine. Methods. We compiled multifrequency radio data for 143 blazars and fitted their radio spectra with a combined power-law and SSA model to derive the turnover frequency and turnover flux density. The magnetic field strengths were then calculated by combining the SSA parameters with the very long baseline interferometry (VLBI) measured angular sizes and Doppler factors compiled from the literature. Results. The SSA-derived magnetic fields are systematically lower than those obtained from broadband spectral energy distribution modeling, suggesting that the two methods probe different regions of the jet. We further investigated the correlations between the SSA magnetic field strength and the apparent knot speed, {\gamma}-ray luminosity, black hole mass, and accretion disk luminosity. Only a weak correlation is found between the SSA magnetic field strength and {\gamma}-ray luminosity, while the best-fit relations with the other investigated parameters also exhibit negative slopes, suggesting a common tendency that is qualitatively consistent with magnetic energy being progressively converted into bulk kinetic energy during jet propagation. Conclusions. Our work presents a large statistical studies of SSA-derived magnetic fields in blazars and provides new observational constraints on the magnetic evolution of relativistic jets.

**Abstract:** Electron-recoil directionality can help distinguish solar-neutrino interactions from backgrounds and, together with recoil-energy measurements and three-dimensional tracking, enable neutrino-energy spectroscopy. We study the reconstruction of the initial direction of low-energy electron recoils in the CYGNO optical time-projection chamber, focusing on the 50 L LIME prototype and its high-granularity scientific CMOS (sCMOS) readout. A dedicated algorithm identifies the interaction point from the asymmetry of the track light distribution and estimates the recoil direction from the early portion of the ionization trail, before multiple scattering erases most of the directional information. The method is characterized with a detailed Monte Carlo simulation of electron recoils and detector response. Samples spanning 15 keV to 70 keV are used to optimize the algorithm parameters by minimizing the width of the reconstructed-minus-true projected-angle distribution. The projected angular response is modeled by two Gaussian components that account for its dependence on track inclination. The narrower component, associated with favorable projections approximately parallel to the Gas Electron Multiplier (GEM) plane, has a width that improves from about $20^\circ$ near 20 keV to about $12^\circ$ at 60 keV. We also quantify the fraction of events in the correctly reconstructed angular core and discuss the dominant limitations of a camera-only two-dimensional measurement.

**Abstract:** We analyze the microlensing event OGLE-2018-BLG-0829, whose light curve exhibits a weak short-term anomaly in addition to the prominent caustic-crossing features of a binary lens. The anomaly cannot be explained by a binary-lens single-source (2L1S) model. We therefore investigate both binary-lens binary-source (2L2S) and triple-lens single-source (3L1S) interpretations and find that both models reproduce the additional anomaly, with the 3L1S model being favored by $\Delta\chi^2=15.1$. Under the favored 3L1S interpretation, the anomaly arises from a caustic induced by a planetary-mass companion, whereas the prominent caustic-crossing features are produced by the underlying binary lens. Combining the measured event timescale, $t_{\rm E}=7.55\pm0.30$ days, and angular Einstein radius, $\theta_{\rm E}=0.325 \pm 0.059$ mas, with a Bayesian analysis, we infer a lens system consisting of an M dwarf of mass $0.193^{+0.300}_{-0.098}\,M_\odot$, a possible brown-dwarf companion of mass $0.068^{+0.105}_{-0.034}\,M_\odot$, and a Saturn-mass planet of mass $0.337^{+0.522}_{-0.171}\,M_{\rm J}$. The companions have projected separations of approximately 3.6 au and 1.6 au from the primary, respectively. However, the physical interpretation remains tentative because potentially important higher-order effects have not been considered, and the dynamical stability of the proposed configuration remains uncertain. If confirmed, this would be the third star--brown dwarf--planet system identified through microlensing, highlighting the potential of microlensing to detect planets in complex multiple systems.

## 90. [Population structure of the velocity distribution in normal shock waves: Gaussian representations, Mach-number similarity, and prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20302) <a id="link90"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20302

**Authors:** Ehsan Roohi

**Abstract:** Normal shock waves are commonly described as mixtures of upstream and downstream Maxwellians. Discrete-velocity solutions of three relaxation models up to Mach 12 and direct simulation Monte Carlo (DSMC) of hard-sphere and variable-hard-sphere gases up to Mach 25 are used to examine the population structure, Mach-number similarity and upstream precursor of their velocity distributions. Three anisotropic Gaussian populations reproduce the streamwise reduced distributions of the Bhatnagar--Gross--Krook (BGK) model and of DSMC to within 4\% up to Mach 12, and of DSMC to within 6\% at Mach 25, whereas two Maxwellians incur errors up to 50\% (BGK) and 37\% (DSMC). Collision histories identify the hard-sphere beam with unscattered stream molecules, which carry half of the peak viscous stress; the other populations are effective descriptions, not collision classes. Above Mach 5, Rankine--Hugoniot-scaled distributions are nearly Mach-independent, with corrections linear in $1/M^2$, consistent with the scaled kinetic equation. At equal normalised density, Mach 8 and 12 give the heat flux and stress at Mach 25 within 1\%, comparable to the accuracy of the reference; with one moment equation for position, physical-space profiles follow within 5\%. Comparing three prediction methods shows that the accuracy comes from the scaling; the Gaussian form adds compactness. Ahead of the shock, the mass of upstream-moving molecules decays as a stretched exponential with effective exponent 0.68--0.87. Collisions regenerate upstream-moving molecules, which explains a decay slower than free-flight survival; a linear cascade model with a downstream-Maxwellian source and no input from the shock interior reproduces the decay rates within 1.2\%.

## 91. [Multi-year forecasting of F10.7 and F30 solar radio flux using an adaptive Kalman filter and hemispheric solar activity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19947) <a id="link91"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19947

**Authors:** Zaina Abu-Shaar, Tatiana Podladchikova, Astrid M. Veronig, Stefan J. Hofmeister, Anatoly Petrukovich

**Abstract:** The F10.7 and F30 radio flux indices drive thermospheric and radiation-environment models, and long-term predictions are needed for satellite mission planning, orbital-lifetime estimates, and re-entry assessments. We develop multi-year predictions of both indices for lead times of 1 to 84 months using three complementary contributions. First, we apply the McNish-Lincoln (M&L) method and demonstrate its predictive skill over multi-year horizons, with RMSE of 11-22 sfu for F10.7 and 7-14 sfu for F30. RMSE largely stabilizes after roughly 36 months, indicating near-constant forecast skill over the seven-year horizon. Accuracy also depends strongly on the solar-cycle phase at which the forecast is issued, with the most favorable starting intervals occurring progressively earlier in the cycle for longer lead times. Second, we update the initial predictions using an adaptive Kalman filter with noise statistics identification, which assimilates the latest monthly observations to reconstruct the current smoothed flux, remove the delay introduced by centered smoothing, and improve solar-cycle state estimation, reducing forecast errors by up to 70%. Third, we introduce synthetic hemispheric components of solar radio flux to account for the separate evolution of the northern and southern hemispheres, and incorporate them into a hemispheric M&L formulation. Compared with the initial M&L predictions, it reduces RMSE by up to 55% for F10.7 and 58% for F30, showing that hemispheric asymmetry contains predictive information lost in the full-disk index. These results extend solar-cycle forecasting to multi-year timescales relevant for long-term space-weather applications and operational planning.

## 92. [Disentangling Magnetospheric and Colliding-Wind X-ray and Radio Emission in the Post-Mass-Transfer Magnetic Massive Binary Plaskett's Star](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19567) <a id="link92"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19567

**Authors:** Ayan Biswas, Gregg A. Wade, Maurice Leutenegger, Poonam Chandra, Yael Naze, Barnali Das, Veronique Petit

**Abstract:** Plaskett's star is an O+O binary system harboring a kG-order magnetic field associated with the broad-lined secondary. It is the only O-type star believed to host a centrifugally supported magnetosphere. We present orbital- and rotational-phase resolved X-ray and low-frequency radio observations of Plaskett's star. X-ray observations were taken with Chandra using the High Energy Transmission Grating Spectrometer (HETGS), while the radio observations come from uGMRT and VLA. The X-ray light curve shows rotational modulation according to the magnetic component's rotation period, with maximum flux near magnetic minima. We confirm the decline in flux near orbital phase 0.5 where the slow rotator primary is in front of its magnetic companion. We used the $f/i$ ratios of Si XIII and Mg XI from high-resolution X-ray spectroscopy to infer the location of the plasma emitting X-rays. We find no statistically significant variation in line widths or centroid shifts with either orbital or rotational phase; however, given instrumental limitations, this non-detection may partly reflect observational constraints, while still suggesting a complex and extended X-ray emitting plasma distribution. We could not detect the star in the low-frequency radio bands (0.6--1.5 GHz). Re-analysis of existing Very Large Array data reveals an additional detection at 3 GHz, consistent with a nonthermal contribution predicted by magnetospheric models, although no direct signature was obtained. At low frequencies, nonthermal emission from the magnetosphere and the colliding-wind region is likely suppressed by free-free absorption in the winds of one or both components.

## 93. [Impact of the Initial Lorentz Factor on Kinetic Instabilities, Magnetic-Field Evolution, and Particle Acceleration in Relativistic Jets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21109) <a id="link93"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21109

**Authors:** Ram Chandra Pageni, Kenichi Nishikawa, Athina Meli, Ioana Dutan, Jacob There Frederiksen, Christoph Koehn, Nicholas R. MacDonald, Yosuke Mizuno

**Abstract:** We investigate how the initial Lorentz factor shapes the evolution of relativistic jets---a key open question in high-energy astrophysics. Using global three-dimensional particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations with cylindrical jet geometry on a Cartesian grid, we analyze the self-consistent evolution of relativistic electron--positron and electron--ion jets from active galactic nuclei (AGN) and their interaction with the ambient medium. We implement an injection scheme that assigns space- and time-dependent velocities to injected particles and simulate jets with initial Lorentz factors of $\gamma_{0} = 10$ and 15. Jets with the higher Lorentz factor ($\gamma_{0} = 15$) exhibit stronger electron collimation, whereas those with $\gamma_{0} = 10$ show larger growth rates of kinetic instabilities during the linear stage. In both cases, a non-oscillatory electric field accelerates and decelerates charged particles throughout the linear and nonlinear stages. Upon entering the nonlinear regime, the initial toroidal magnetic field undergoes significant amplification driven by the Weibel instability (WI), the kinetic Kelvin--Helmholtz instability (kKHI), and the mushroom instability (MI), and subsequently reorganizes into complex topologies. In the late nonlinear stage, magnetic islands with randomly swirling fields indicate a highly turbulent magnetic structure. These findings demonstrate that global 3D~PIC modeling is essential for capturing the interplay among kinetic instabilities, magnetic turbulence, and particle acceleration in relativistic outflows, bridging microscopic plasma physics with macroscopic observations of AGN jets and gamma-ray bursts.

**Novelty:** 4

## 94. [Advancing Electroformed X-ray Optics Bridging Synchrotron Radiation Technology and Space Astronomy: Improved Figure Accuracy and X-ray Imaging Performancev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20343) <a id="link94"></a>

**Authors:** Ikuyuki Mitsuishi, Yusuke Yoshida, Kazuki Ampuku, Makoto Yoshihara, Keitoku Yoshihira, Tetsuo Kano, Naoki Ishida, Koki Sakuta, Noriyuki Narukage, Keisuke Tamura, Kikuko Miyata, Gota Yamaguchi, Hidekazu Takano, Yoshiki Kohmura, Shutaro Mohri, Takehiro Kume, Yusuke Matsuzawa, Yoichi Imamura, Takahiro Saito, Kentaro Hiraguri, Hirokazu Hashizume, Hidekazu Mimura

**Abstract:** We have developed high-resolution electroformed nickel X-ray telescope mirrors by extending precision mirror-fabrication techniques developed for synchrotron X-ray microscopy to space astronomy. Our previous study identified axial figure errors of a replicated mirror as one of the factors limiting its X-ray angular resolution. In the present study, we fabricated a new mirror using a substantially improved mandrel and directly compared the two-dimensional figure errors of the mandrels and replicated inner mirror surfaces for both the previous and new mandrel--mirror pairs. This comparison reveals how contributions to the final mirror figure errors changed with improved mandrel accuracy. For the previous mirror, the mandrel and mirror showed a strong spatial correlation in their figure errors, indicating that a substantial component of the mirror figure pattern was inherited from the mandrel. In contrast, after improving the mandrel figure, the new mirror exhibited a weaker mandrel--mirror spatial correlation, while the residual figure deviations arising between the mandrel and final mirror became comparable in magnitude to the residual mandrel figure errors. The improved mandrel reduced the central 68% widths, W68, of the axial slope error distribution from 3.3 to 1.6 arcsec for the paraboloidal surface and from 5.0 to 1.1 arcsec for the hyperboloidal surface. Correspondingly, the mirror values improved from 4.6 to 2.4 arcsec and from 12.4 to 2.0 arcsec, respectively. X-ray measurements at 12 keV yielded local half power diameters (HPDs) of approximately 7--17 arcsec and a full-aperture HPD of 8.5 arcsec. The newly fabricated mirror was adopted for the soft X-ray telescope of the fifth Focusing Optics X-ray Solar Imager (FOXSI-5) sounding rocket flight and was successfully flown in May 2026.

## 95. [Non-Canonical Q-balls in Polyattractor Inflation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21413) <a id="link95"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21413

**Authors:** Kimberley Siret, Rhys Abercromby, Kit Lloyd-Stubbs, John McDonald

**Abstract:** We show that Q-balls can exist in supergravity-based polyattractor inflation models that interpolate between $\alpha$-attractor T-models and polynomial attractor models. We consider both the original polyattractor potential and a natural generalisation. We find that Q-balls solutions exist when the model deviates significantly from the T-model $\alpha$-attractor, whereas in the T-model limit no Q-balls exist. These are non-canonical Q-balls due to the non-canonical kinetic term of the complex inflaton field. Such Q-balls could have significant cosmological consequences if they are produced at the end of inflation. In particular, if the global $\mathrm{U}(1)$ symmetry of the supergravity-based polyattractor model is unbroken, the Q-balls can be absolutely stable and could account for dark matter.

**Abstract:** Polarization measurements of radio transients, particularly their frequency dependence, shed light on the magnetic environments of these sources. We extend the analytical solution of radiative transfer of the polarization properties of such transients to both a cold magnetized plasmas beyond the high-frequency approximation and a relativistic plasma with synchrotron emission. The Faraday mixing coefficients are enhanced makes Stokes parameters $Q$, $U$, and $V$ to exhibit a more rapid and complex frequency-dependent evolution. A relativistic plasma medium can also generate both Faraday roataion and conversion with a smaller magnetic field. The relativistic plasma is investigated as a persistent radio source (PRS) associated with FRB, and it is found that, although the observed PRS luminosity can be accounted for, an FRB can propagate out only under certain special parameter conditions. We apply the model to fit the polarization profile of GPM J1839$-$10 and revisit some FRBs. All sources with Faraday conversion require that the environment's magnetic field not exceed 100~G. The magnetic field of FRB 20180301A and GPM J1839$-$10 are almost perpendicular to the light of sight for at least some times.

## 97. [XRISM/Xtend detection of solar wind charge exchange induced by multiple coronal mass ejections in 2024 August](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20934) <a id="link97"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20934

**Authors:** Daijiro Kanno, Daiki Ishi, Yuichiro Ezoe, Masaki Numazawa, Kumi Ishikawa, Shogo B Kobayashi, Satoru Katsuda, Liyi Gu

**Abstract:** Solar wind charge exchange (SWCX) emission is often a major component of the soft X-ray background, but it also provides a remote diagnostic of the composition and ionization state of solar wind plasma. We present an analysis of a SWCX event observed with XRISM/Xtend during 2024 August 9-13. The event occurred during intense solar activity with multiple flares, including an X1.3-class solar flare, and associated coronal mass ejections (CMEs). We found a clear enhancement in the 0.3-3.0 keV count rate that occurred nearly simultaneously with an increase in the solar wind proton flux measured by ACE. We fitted the spectrum during the enhancement period with a set of Gaussian emission lines. In addition to the commonly detected C, N, and O lines, we detected Ne, Mg, Si, and Fe lines at $>15\sigma$ significance. We extracted two characteristic periods within the enhanced interval based on the CME arrival timeline. The O$_{\rm VIII}$/Si$_{\rm XIII}$ line ratio changed significantly between the two periods, suggesting contributions from multiple CME components. We then applied the AtomDB charge exchange model with a multi-temperature setup. The estimated ionization temperatures for Ne, Mg, Si, and Fe increased with the peak soft X-ray flux of the associated solar flares. This trend suggests that coronal heating becomes more efficient for more intense flares. Meanwhile, the ionization temperatures were lower than typical flare electron temperatures, implying that CME plasma may have been released into interplanetary space under non-equilibrium ionization conditions. We also found an indication of an inverse-FIP pattern, with reduced relative abundances of Si and S. These results demonstrate that XRISM/Xtend can diagnose the coronal plasma through SWCX emission, including heavy ions such as Ne, Mg, and Si, which are difficult to measure with current in-situ instruments (e.g., ACE satellite).
