## 98. [PeV Gamma-Ray Emitters: Observations and Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20542) <a id="link98"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20542

**Authors:** Ruo-Yu Liu

**Abstract:** The origin of Galactic cosmic rays (CRs) around and beyond the knee remains an open problem. Gamma rays extending to the PeV range provide direct evidence for extreme particle acceleration. LHAASO has greatly expanded the observational sample of such sources. In this review, we use the term "PeV gamma-ray emitter" for a source whose measured spectrum extends to, or approaches, 1 PeV, rather than restricting it to sources detected above 1 PeV at more than $5\sigma$. We review LHAASO observations of six PeV gamma-ray emitters (i.e., the Crab Nebula, the Aquila Booster, the Cygnus Bubble, Cygnus X-3, V4641 Sgr, and GRS 1915+105) together with relevant multiwavelength observations and on-going theoretical studies. Together, these sources show that PeV particle acceleration can occur in different environments and on spatial scales ranging from compact pulsar-wind nebulae and binary-scale jets to large-scale jet--environment interaction regions and extended star-forming complexes. Their spectra, morphologies, variability, magnetic fields, and surrounding gas provide important constraints on particle acceleration and transport mechanisms. We also discuss the implications of these observations for the origin of Galactic PeV CRs and future observational prospects for clarifying this problem.

## 99. [Mid-Infrared Variability as a Probe of Hidden AGN Activity: A Comparison of Seyfert 1 and Seyfert 2 AGNs in the BAT 58-Month Sample](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18904) <a id="link99"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18904

**Authors:** Natalie Kovacevic, Lorelei Starling, Xinyu Dai, Emilia Jaervelae

**Abstract:** In accordance with the AGN Unification model, the optical variability of Seyfert 2s is expected to be suppressed because the central engine is obscured by a dusty torus. The absorbed UV/optical radiation is reprocessed and re-emitted in longer wavelengths including the mid-infrared (MIR), making MIR variability a promising probe of obscured AGNs. We investigate the optical ASAS-SN and MIR NEOWISE variability of Seyfert 1 and Seyfert 2 AGNs from the \textit{Swift} 58-month BAT catalog, comparing both populations against non-variable galaxies. One of the first systematic studies of MIR AGN variability, we detected significant variability for Sy1s and the majority of Sy2s. 65% of Sy2s exhibit variability as significant as Sy1s in the MIR, especially in the W2 band, indicating torus dominated MIR variability. Additionally, 22% of Sy2s show higher variability compared to non-variable sources in the W2 band with p-value of $7.1\times 10^{-15}$. Demonstrating MIR variability as an important method to probe the variability of torus and select obscured AGN. Finally, we perform dust reverberation mapping on the Sy1 sample between the optical and W1 or W2 light curves, with measured time delays between 22-786 days (0.02-0.61 pc) for the hot dust in the inner regions of the torus. Our measured torus radius-luminosity slope, $R \propto L^{0.42\pm0.05}$, agrees with literature results. Combining the W1 and W2 band lags with archival NIR measurements, we measured the temperature profile of the torus with $T \propto R^{-\alpha}$, where $\alpha$ values range from 0.3-1.1.

## 100. [Beam-pattern Prediction for Radio Antenna Arrays using Mutual Coupling and Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18968) <a id="link100"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18968

**Authors:** Anna M. Heckel, Chris J. Pate, Jungmin Kim, Peter T. Timbie, Zongfu Yu

**Abstract:** Radio antennas are often used to form arrays, such as phased arrays and interferometer arrays. For most applications the beam patterns of these instruments must be accurately known. For phased arrays the array patterns are formed by coherently adding the embedded element patterns (EEPs) of the individual antennas in the array. For interferometers, the fundamental measurement is of visibilities, which depend on the products of pairs of EEPs. Unfortunately, in either case, for closely-spaced arrays, the EEPs can be distorted by mutual coupling between the antennas and can be very different from the isolated element patterns (IEPs), measured when the antennas are far apart. Mutual coupling is characterized by the scattering parameters for the array. Full-wave electromagnetic simulations can simulate the EEPs, including mutual coupling effects, but these simulations can be computationally expensive and require accurate knowledge of the design of the array elements, their placement, and the environment in which they are deployed. Directly measuring either the EEPs or the array patterns for instruments in the field is often impractical as well. In this paper we show that in cases where the scattering parameters between array elements can be measured, they can be used to infer the array patterns of a phased array. We demonstrate the training of a deep neural network to simulate array patterns when given: the design of the antennas, the locations of the antennas, the frequency of operation, and measurements of the scattering parameters of the array. As a demonstration for a simple model, we apply this approach to the simulation of the array patterns for a small phased array of three dipole antennas and evaluate the sensitivity of this procedure to uncertainties in measurements of the antenna positions and scattering parameters.

**Abstract:** For low background experiments, prediction of the background from radioactivity in detector components is of critical importance. This part-to-part variation can have an impact on the total activity and is not presently captured by the averaging methods used to evaluate radioassays. We propose a new method for the averaging of radioassays to incorporate this part-to-part variation into predictions of the total activity of parts. We also consider how to add component activities based on assays for component groups that are simulated as a single entity.

## 102. [Drift of a Charged Particle in a Black Hole Magnetosphere: the Effect of Black Hole Spin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19098) <a id="link102"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19098

**Authors:** Ye Shen

**Abstract:** Sparse plasma immersed in a strong magnetic field is common in astrophysical scenarios, for which a collisionless description is more appropriate. The guiding-center approximation serves as an effective tool for describing the motion of charged particles in a strong magnetic field, revealing the drift, a sub-leading order motion that causes particles to deviate from the magnetic field lines. It is widely accepted that the charged particles drift under the induction of gravity in Newtonian framework. However, a covariant formulation of the guiding-center approximation, as well as the drift of a charged particle induced by strong gravitational field, has yet to be fully explored. As a preliminary study, we aim to analyze the drift of a single charged particle in a black hole magnetosphere via a covariant approach. Particularly, we focus on the effects of black hole spin. We first examine the drift caused by the rotation of a massive object in the Newtonian regime using the language of gravitoelectromagnetism. A covariant formula of drift is derived, which is confirmed to reduce to the Newtonian results in the weak gravitational field and low speed limit. The impact of BH spin on the drift is analyzed in detail in Kerr spacetime specifically, for which we define the drift rate whose value quantifies the significance of the drift. Our results indicate that the impact of BH spin on the drift of a charged particle in a BH magnetosphere can be comparable to that of other factors in some extreme cases.

**Authors:** Giulia Piccinini, Claudia Di Maio, Giuseppina Micela, Jonathan Brande, David R. Ciardi, Catherine A. Clark, Ian J. M. Crossfield, Julian C. van Eyken, Miranda Felsmann, Emily A. Gilbert, Michael B. Lund, Rachel A. Matson, Elisabeth C. Matthews, Alex S. Polanski, Joshua E. Schlieder

**Abstract:** One of the key goals of planetary population studies is to achieve broad coverage of the parameter space, which is not yet fully represented by confirmed planets. This limitation can be mitigated by including planets that have been statistically validated through a vetting procedure, even if they are not fully confirmed. In preparation for the Ariel mission, a Mission Candidate Sample has been assembled, comprising both confirmed planets and several unvalidated candidates. In this context, we apply a vetting analysis to a selected subsample of these candidates. Our methodology is based on the TRICERATOPS tool, aimed at confirming the presence of transiting planets or ruling out false-positive scenarios. We applied TRICERATOPS to a sample of 318 targets drawn from the last three Ariel TESS Planet Candidates catalogues. The analysis includes evaluation of statistical probabilities, as well as checks on stellar binarity indicators such as the Renormalized Unit Weight Error (RUWE) and the IPD_frac_multi_peak values from Gaia Data Release 3. Our analysis validated 56 candidates as planets, identified 29 false positives, and classified 35 as likely planets. These results are intended to support the Ariel mission by providing a probabilistic priority ranking to guide follow-up observations and optimise targets for atmospheric characterisation studies.

**Abstract:** Kepler recorded several thousand dedicated sky-background pixels per detector output every 29.4 min from 2009 to 2013, an archive that we use to study comets. ## 106. [X-ray Flare Evolution and Changing-Look Nature of the TeV Blazar OQ 334](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21518) <a id="link106"></a>

**Abstract:** The blazar OQ 334 (B2 1420+326, z = 0.682) is one of the few jetted AGN with repeated changing-look (CL) transitions between FSRQ and BL Lac states. We present a multi-wavelength study covering 2017 December to 2024 September, focusing on three X-ray flares observed with Swift-XRT (X1: 2019 June 26--July 24, X2: 2020 January 4--February 21, X3: 2024 June 24--August 2) and on the spectral properties of the CL states. The spectrum hardens from flare to flare, with $\Gamma$ decreasing from $1.97 \pm 0.09$ to $1.71 \pm 0.04$ and $1.34 \pm 0.06$, while the hysteresis evolves from an initial clockwise loop, consistent with cooling-dominated emission, to multi-loop trajectories in the later flares. The X-ray spectra separate the CL states, with FSRQ states hardest ($\Gamma_X \approx 1.3$--$1.5$), BL Lac states softest ($\Gamma_X \approx 1.8$--$2.4$), and transition states intermediate. Within the CL states, flux and photon index are positively correlated ($r = 0.59$, $p = 2.5 \times 10^{-2}$), producing a softer-when-brighter trend. The first two flares coincide with FSRQ $\rightarrow$ BL Lac transitions, whereas the third, the brightest, remains FSRQ-like. One-zone leptonic modelling of the broadband SEDs reproduces the flares with a weak, nearly constant magnetic field and a particle-dominated region, and the X-ray emission evolves from a substantial synchrotron contribution in the first flare to complete inverse-Compton dominance in the third. The X-ray band traces the changing balance between the synchrotron and inverse-Compton components in the flares and provides a sensitive probe of the CL behaviour of the source.

## 107. [Linear Electrostatic Instabilities Driven by Relativistic Non-Maxwellian Pair Beams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21041) <a id="link107"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21041

**Authors:** Suman Dey

**Abstract:** We investigate how non-Maxwellian momentum distributions affect the linear growth of electrostatic instabilities driven by relativistic pair beams, motivated by the lack of a universally accepted analytical description of blazar-induced pair beams. Different beam distributions are used to describe relativistic pair beams in analytical studies and particle-in-cell simulations. A systematic comparison is therefore needed to determine how the beam distribution influences the instability growth rates. We formulate the relativistic electrostatic response in the weak-beam limit for an anisotropic $\kappa$-type distribution and compare the resulting growth rates with those obtained for Gaussian and Maxwell--J\"uttner distributions. We require $\kappa>3/2$ for a finite root-mean-square momentum spread. For the parameters considered, the $\kappa$-type distributions yield higher maximum growth rates than the Gaussian distribution, even when their mean momenta and momentum spreads are the same. The fastest-growing modes correspond to parallel propagation for the $\kappa$ and Gaussian distributions, whereas the Maxwell--J\"uttner distribution exhibits its maximum for oblique propagation for a mildly relativistic beam. An additional calculation for a highly relativistic pair beam with $\kappa=2$ finds an oblique maximum of the electrostatic growth rate. The beam momentum distribution influences the linear growth of electrostatic instabilities under the conditions investigated.

These observations were taken with the MASK335R coronagraph mask and in dual-filter F444W/F200W mode. Our library consists of 247 observations (counting both filters separately) of 100 different stars of spectral types mostly F to M, with a total of 1110 individual integrations. We explore the PSF subtraction hyper-parameters with the KLIP algorithm (Soummer et al. 2012) that maximize sensitivity, and we show the performance of our library compared to and in addition to contemporaneous reference observations. We show that our library improves sensitivity at close separations in most tested cases, with improvement factors from $\sim1.3$ to $\sim2$ at some separations. As practical recommendations, we find that our library is best used as a complement to contemporaneous reference observations to further improve sensitivity, since in all our experiments contemporaneous references are needed for best sensitivity. **Abstract:** We present a photometric study of ASASSN-15af (TIC~231050794), combining TESS full-frame-image photometry from the TEQUILA project with 2-minute SPOC light curves and archival ASAS-SN and ATLAS monitoring over 4400~d. The long-term light curve shows recurrent dwarf-nova outbursts, establishing ASASSN-15af as a high-inclination eclipsing dwarf nova above the canonical period gap. From 391 quiescent eclipses, we derive the working ephemeris $T(E)=2139.76108(3)+0.14982063(2)\,E$ (BTJD), corresponding to $P_{\rm orb}=3.596$~hr. A quadratic fit is formally better, but the timings sample only three widely separated epochs, so we regard the apparent O--C curvature as tentative. In quiescence, the out-of-eclipse waveform is dominated by a stable $2f_{\rm orb}$ component, consistent with a substantial ellipsoidal contribution from the donor; during the Sector~96 outburst, its fractional amplitude decreases by $\simeq66\%$, consistent with dilution by the brightened disk. The outburst-only signals occur at periods $\sim1.6\%$ shorter than $P_{\rm orb}$ in Sector~2 and $\sim3.1\%$ longer in Sector~96, consistent with negative- and positive-superhump-like modulations, respectively. Because each is detected in only one outburst, their recurrence remains to be established. Their period-offset ratio, $\phi\simeq0.51$, is consistent with empirical trends among systems showing both modes, while the inferred superhump-based mass ratios span $q\simeq0.06$--$0.24$. During the Sector~96 outburst, the eclipse deepens by $\Delta D=0.196\pm0.028$ and its centroid shifts earlier by $\Delta t_{\rm c}=-66.6\pm7.3$~s, indicating a redistribution of accretion-powered light. These results identify ASASSN-15af as an above-gap eclipsing dwarf nova with a dynamically variable accretion disk; spectroscopy is needed to constrain the mass ratio.

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19989

**Authors:** Hao-Kun Chen, Xu-Lin Dong, Qi-Ling Chen, Shu-Wang Cui, Yi-Qing Guo

**Abstract:** In recent years, cosmic-ray observations have entered a stage of high-precision measurements, but the phases of large-scale anisotropy reported by different experiments differ significantly, partly because the first harmonic cannot fully describe non-dipolar structures. In this work, we process the one-dimensional relative-intensity distributions from ARGO-YBJ, Tibet AS$\gamma$, and HAWC using a unified procedure, perform multi-harmonic fits, and reconstruct the distributions, with the global maximum of each distribution used to define the peak amplitude $A_{\max}$ and the peak right ascension $\alpha_{\max}$. The results show that, in the common energy range of 11--22~TeV covered by the three experiments, the range of the published first-harmonic phases is $22.6^\circ$, whereas the range of $\alpha_{\max}$ is reduced to $7.5^\circ$; $\alpha_{\max}$ evolves overall from $60^\circ$--$70^\circ$ at the low-energy end to $250^\circ$--$300^\circ$ at the high-energy end, while $A_{\max}$ exhibits a local minimum and $\alpha_{\max}$ changes rapidly in the phase-flip region. At the low-energy end, $\alpha_{\max}$ lies between the right-ascension projections of the direction toward the Geminga supernova remnant and the direction of the local interstellar magnetic field. Accordingly, we construct a spatially dependent propagation model that includes Galactic background sources, a Geminga-related nearby source, and anisotropic diffusion along the direction of the local interstellar magnetic field. Within a unified framework, the model reproduces the energy spectra of P, He, C, O, and Fe, as well as the energy evolution of $A_{\max}$ and $\alpha_{\max}$, providing a self-consistent explanation for the origin of large-scale anisotropy in the TeV to tens-of-TeV energy range.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 4

## 111. [3D Radiative Transfer Simulations for Characterization of the Optically Thick DART Ejecta Plume](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18984) <a id="link111"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18984

**Authors:** Yaeji Kim, Lioudmila Kolokolova, Tony L. Farnham, Sabina D. Raducan, Ramin Lolachi, David A. Glenar, Timothy J. Stubbs

**Abstract:** The Hyperion package is a three-dimensional Monte-Carlo radiative transfer approach to modeling dust ejecta. Because it has proven to be a powerful tool for simulating optically thick environments with complicated structure, we are applying the routines to observations of the DART impact on asteroid Dimorphos. Our analysis focuses on the LICIACube LUKE red-filter image acquired 175 s after impact, when the near-surface plume remained dense and not strongly structured. The model incorporates the updated Dimorphos shape model, spacecraft and solar geometry derived, and size-dependent scattering properties of realistic dust particles. We adopt an axisymmetric conical ejecta geometry and iteratively adjust the particle size distribution (PSD) and vertical number density profile to reproduce observed brightness profiles extracted from the LUKE image. We find that a power-law PSD with slope k = 3.0 and a number density peak located at approximately 120 m above the surface best reproduces the brightness contrast between the illuminated surface, the bright ejecta plume, and the adjacent dark band produced by shadowing within the optically thick material. The resulting best-fit model implies a total ejecta mass of 1.25 x 10^6 kg within 500 m of the impact site.

## 112. [Radial Evolution of a Corotating Interaction Region from a High-Resolution MHD Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19438) <a id="link112"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19438

**Authors:** Mengxuan Ma, Liping Yang, Fang Shen, Xueshang Feng, Xinyi Ma, Wangning Zhang, Xiao Zhang

**Abstract:** Corotating interaction regions (CIRs) are large-scale structures formed by fast--slow solar wind interaction, playing a crucial role in heliospheric dynamics and space weather. However, the evolution of individual CIRs and their full three-dimensional morphology remain poorly understood. We investigate the radial evolution of a single CIR using a high-resolution simulation combined with a backmapping method. The simulation reproduces the major plasma and magnetic signatures observed by Wind at 1~AU and by MAVEN and Tianwen-1 at $\sim$1.6~AU. The CIR exhibits a warped, shell-like spiral structure tilted relative to the heliospheric current sheet, consistent with theoretical predictions. During its developing stage, geometric changes are modest, and the radial scalings of velocity ($V_r \propto r^{0.02}$), density ($N \propto r^{-2.00}$), magnetic field ($B_{\rm total} \propto r^{-1.74}$), and temperature ($T \propto r^{-0.97}$) are comparable to those of the background solar wind. In the mature stage, the CIR drifts in longitude and expands toward high latitudes, while its longitudinal and latitudinal widths decrease with distance. The full-domain scalings change markedly to $V_r \propto r^{0.13}$, $N \propto r^{-1.91}$, $B_{\rm total} \propto r^{0.35}$, and $T \propto r^{-0.52}$, differing from both the background wind and the ecliptic-plane CIR. This study quantifies the radial evolution of a CIR from formation to maturity and highlights the importance of its three-dimensional structure in characterizing its morphology and dynamics.

## 113. [Non-thermal emission from molecular clouds illuminated by continuous cosmic-ray accelerators: application to the stellar cluster W43](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21317) <a id="link113"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21317

**Authors:** Alexandre Inventar, Enrico Peretti, Naomi Tsuji, Stefano Gabici

**Abstract:** Context: Cosmic rays escaping from their accelerators produce gamma-ray emission through interactions with ambient gas. While the scenario of an impulsive injection of cosmic rays has been extensively studied, that of a continuous injection in time has received comparatively less attention. The latter scenario is particularly relevant for stellar clusters, which may contribute to a non-negligible fraction of Galactic cosmic rays. Aims: We intend to characterize the excesses of cosmic rays around their accelerators and to assess the detectability of the gamma-ray emission resulting from the interactions of cosmic rays in nearby molecular clouds. We focus in particular on star clusters and seek to apply these considerations to the case of W43. Methods: We derive analytical estimates for the cosmic ray excess around sources as a function of source properties and transport parameters. We also evaluate under which conditions the predicted gamma-ray emission from illuminated molecular clouds can be detected by current and future instruments. We then apply these results to explain the gamma-ray emission from the the W43 region. Results: We find that producing detectable gamma-ray emission from molecular clouds illuminated by cosmic rays escaping from star clusters generally requires either suppressed diffusion relative to the Galactic value or anisotropic diffusion, with the latter scenario requiring a smaller global energetic. We applied this approach to the case of W43 and by fitting its gamma-ray emission we were able to constrain the underlying CR transport parameters.

**Abstract:** We present a nonperturbative lattice study of vacuum decay through bubble nucleation in a three-dimensional real scalar field theory at finite temperature. The vacuum decay rate is computed by combining an expanded-ensemble multicanonical hybrid Monte Carlo approach, which samples the critical bubble configurations, with real-time Langevin dynamics, which follows the subsequent bubble evolution. We extrapolate the nucleation rate to the continuum limit and compare it with the corresponding one-loop perturbative prediction, finding a discrepancy of about \(8\%\).

## 115. [Astrophysical Constraints for Bosonic Dark Matter in and Around Quark Stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21368) <a id="link115"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21368

**Authors:** Júlia Tartera Barberà, Sarah Louisa Pitz, Jürgen Schaffner-Bielich, Laura Tolos

**Abstract:** We investigate the structure, stability, and mass-radius relations of compact stars composed of quark matter within the non-ideal MIT Bag Model mixed with self-interacting bosonic dark matter with a $\phi^n$ self-interaction potential. By solving the coupled two-fluid Tolman-Oppenheimer-Volkoff equations for boson masses between 200 MeV and 1 GeV and self-interaction strengths with $n=4$ and $n=40$, we show that strong self-interactions and higher boson masses enhance the stability of compact star configurations at high central pressures, though they reduce maximum overall masses. Ultra-compact solutions with $C \ge 1/3$ capable of mimicking black holes are significantly restricted compared to hadronic models, emerging only for strong self-interactions and dark matter fractions exceeding 90\%. Depending on the parameters, the compact stars form either extended dark matter halos or compact cores: stronger self-interactions and higher boson masses suppress the radial extension to favor compact cores of dark matter. Matching our models against observational constraints highlights PSR J1614$-$2230 and XTE J1814$-$338 as potential window of opportunity for dark matter detection. Notably, XTE J1814$-$338 could confirm a dark sector, given its requirement for light boson masses ($m_b \approx 200\text{--}300\text{ MeV}$) and strongly DM-dominated configurations.

## 116. [Comparative Analysis of Influences of Magnetic Field Inputs on Simulating the 2017 September 10 Solar Energetic Particle Event](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19628) <a id="link116"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19628

**Authors:** Weihao Liu, Nishtha Sachdeva, Lulu Zhao, Igor V. Sokolov, Hongfan Chen, Christina O. Lee, Patrick A. Dunn, Alessandro Bruno, David Lario, Kathryn Whitman, Xianyu Liu, Gábor Tóth, Tamas I. Gombosi, Meng Jin, Robert F. Wimmer-Schweingruber, Robin Ramstad, Jasper S. Halekas, Claudio Corti, Yoshifumi Futaana, Davin E. Larson, Donald M. Hassler, Bent Ehresmann, Shannon Curry

**Abstract:** Physics-based solar energetic particle (SEP) models simulate the ambient solar wind, coronal mass ejections (CMEs), and SEPs using photospheric magnetic field maps as the inner boundary condition. In this study, we select the 2017 September 10 SEP event, which produced the first ground level enhancement observed on the surfaces of both Earth and Mars, to examine how these inputs influence the modeled results. We use the SOlar wind with FIeld lines and Energetic particles (SOFIE) model suite, driven independently by the GONG and ADAPT-GONG magnetic field maps, to simulate the solar wind, CME, and SEPs from the low corona to beyond Mars' orbit. The two maps yield different open flux regions and hence different solar wind conditions, both globally comparable to in situ observations at Earth, STA, and Mars, with the GONG-driven results performing slightly better. We find that in the ADAPT-GONG-driven run, a more energetic CME flux rope propagates into a slower, wider stream, producing broader CME expansion and a more extended shock, which reaches both planets within ~4 hours of the observed arrival, while the GONG-driven run misses both arrivals. Both simulations reproduce the observed SEP fluxes at Earth within a factor of ~3, while the ADAPT-GONG-driven results compare better with observations at Mars, which connects magnetically to the shock flank. Therefore, for this event, the relative performance of the two simulations depends on the observer and observable, with SEP fluxes shaped by CME propagation through the ambient solar wind and by magnetic connectivity to the evolving shock.

## 117. [Noise and quasi-periodic oscillations in black hole accretion flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21246) <a id="link117"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.21246

**Authors:** Emanuele Sobacchi, Luca Comisso

**Abstract:** Temporal fluctuations of the viscosity parameter at different radii in an accretion flow are known to give rise to variability in the accretion rate onto the black hole. The power spectrum of accretion-rate fluctuations is expected to exhibit a broad-band power-law dependence on frequency (e.g., flicker noise). We show that viscosity fluctuations can also generate quasi-periodic oscillations (QPOs) in the power spectrum. In our model, QPOs are caused by reflection of accretion-rate fluctuations at the outer radius of the accretion flow and arise near the low-frequency break of the power spectrum, which corresponds to the inverse viscous timescale at the outer radius. These QPOs occur in geometrically thick advection-dominated accretion flows but are absent in geometrically thin cooling-dominated flows.

## 118. [Distinguishing between flaring and non-flaring solar active regions with height-dependent polarity inversion line lengths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20130) <a id="link118"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20130

**Authors:** Shreeyesh Biswal, Marianna B. Korsos, Manolis K. Georgoulis, Alexander Nindos, Spiros Patsourakos, Robertus Erdelyi

**Abstract:** Polarity Inversion Lines (PILs) are regions on the Sun's surface across which the vertical magnetic field changes polarity. This study examines whether the total length of PILs within a height range of 0-3.24 Mm above the photosphere can distinguish between strongly flaring active regions, with at least one flare of GOES (Geostationary Operational Environmental Satellite) class M1 or higher, and non-flaring active regions, where the strongest flare remains weaker than GOES class C1. Using magnetic field extrapolations on a dataset of 40 active regions, the study compares the 48-hr evolution of the total PIL length prior to the first major flare in strongly flaring regions with identical time intervals in non-flaring regions. It is seen that the 48-hr maximum of the total PIL length differs clearly between the two groups. This distinction becomes stronger at heights about 1-2 Mm above the photosphere. It is further seen that at about 1.8 Mm, the maximum value of the total PIL length, for around (i) 78 percent of weakly flaring regions (where the strongest flare is a C-class flare) and (ii) 96 percent of non-flaring active regions, is less than the minimum value of the total PIL length of their strongly flaring counterparts. These results suggest that strongly flaring active regions may be distinguishable from non-flaring or weakly flaring ones using thresholds on PIL lengths in the lower solar atmosphere. Further, this study highlights the importance of studying chromospheric magnetic fields in flare precursor investigations.

## 119. [High-Resolution DKIST/ViSP Observation Reveals Small-scale Photospheric Magnetic Flux beneath a Coronal Hole Region](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18985) <a id="link119"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18985

**Authors:** Khagendra Katuwal, R. T. James McAteer, Rahul Yadav

**Abstract:** The magnetic unipolarity of photospheric regions beneath coronal holes is well established at spatial resolutions of about 1 arcsecond, but the effect of smaller-scale magnetic structures remains uncertain. We compare co-aligned observations from the Helioseismic and Magnetic Imager (HMI) aboard the Solar Dynamics Observatory and the Visible Spectro-Polarimeter (ViSP) at the Daniel K. Inouye Solar Telescope (DKIST). HMI observes the Fe I 6173 {\AA} line, while ViSP observes the Fe I 6302.5 {\AA} line; both form at similar photospheric heights. We derive the line-of-sight magnetic field (B_LOS) from ViSP spectropolarimetric data using a Milne-Eddington inversion and compare it with the HMI field derived from vector magnetograms over a common coronal-hole region. ViSP detects smaller magnetic structures with stronger fields than HMI. The absolute mean field and mean absolute field are higher by factors of 1.30 and 1.37, respectively. HMI measures approximately 28% less unsigned magnetic flux and 24% less net signed magnetic flux than ViSP. A comparison of spatially degraded ViSP data with HMI indicates that differences in effective resolution and associated spatial averaging contribute to these discrepancies. Nevertheless, the magnetic flux imbalance remains similar: 69% for HMI and 65% for ViSP. Thus, the additional small-scale flux detected by ViSP does not substantially change the measured unipolarity in this region. Because ViSP has a limited field of view, observations covering entire coronal holes are needed to test whether this result holds more generally.

## 120. [Simulating high-speed solar wind streams from coronal holes using an L5-L1 configuration of Lagrangian points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20968) <a id="link120"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20968

**Authors:** Tatiana Podladchikova, Astrid M. Veronig, Manuela Temmer, Stefan J. Hofmeister

**Abstract:** Coronal holes (CHs) are known to be sources of high-speed solar wind streams (HSSs), yet the physical mechanisms linking CH position and characteristics to solar wind (SW) behaviour remain unclear. Our results reveal that the latitude of CHs, especially smaller ones, combined with the heliographic latitude of the solar disk's central point (B0 angle), plays a critical role in driving discrepancies in SW velocity across the heliosphere. To investigate this, we use archival data from STEREO-B, STEREO-A, and Earth to simulate an L5-L1 configuration, where L5 is a vantage point approximately behind Earth in its orbit (as proposed for the Vigil mission), and L1 is between Earth and the Sun where SW measurements are typically taken. We use these insights to develop a predictive algorithm for HSSs, beginning with an analysis of the separation angle and distances between L5 and L1. We then introduce a predictive indicator and empirical criteria based on CH properties and the B0 angle to adjust for changes in SW velocity at L1. Our results show that the L5 viewpoint demonstrates the capability to significantly improve the accuracy and lead times of HSS predictions, enhancing our understanding of the CH-HSS relationship and potentially improving space weather forecasting.

## 121. [On the Prospects of Using Cyclic Spectroscopy on Canonical Pulsars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18882) <a id="link121"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.18882

**Authors:** Lawrence Wise, Jacob Turner

**Abstract:** We use the cyclic figure of merit to determine the likelihood of achieving cyclic deconvolution for 2609 canonical pulsars, a population for which this technique has not previously been used. These pulsars have spin periods between 0.1 and 10 seconds and are surveyed across 15 different telescope-observing frequency combinations. We conclude that cyclic deconvolution will only be a viable method for pulse broadening function or phase retrieval recovery in a handful of canonical pulsars. Few, if any, pulsars pass the conservative threshold for a given telescope-frequency combination. We believe that the cyclic figure of merit does not accurately reflect our ability to use cyclic deconvolution on this population, instead showing a shortcoming of the measurement when observing canonicals at low observing frequencies. Still, it is reliable for MSPs and for canonicals at high observing frequencies. More promising is the prospect of using this technique to recover scintles for canonical pulsars; DSA and FAST are best-suited for this as judged by the number of sources where scintles may be resolved and S/N is sufficient. We suspect there may be more sources that would have large figures of cyclic merit, but insufficient S/N, at very low frequencies. We advocate for expansions of real-time cyclic spectroscopy backends for current and upcoming low frequency telescopes.

The book covers options such as laser power beaming, microwave beaming, a solar-powered climber, and delivering power through the electrical conductivity of the tether. This report starts with the reference conceptual design of a climber from the 2023 study report, "The Climber/Tether Interface of the Space Elevator," and adds features to the climber that allow it to ascend or descend the elevator tether. The strengths and limitations of current technologies are identified, along with areas for future research.

## 123. [The Lukashian Calendar: A universal calendar mechanism using variable-duration years and days for any human colony or settlement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19579) <a id="link123"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19579

**Authors:** Gert-Jan Schouten, Michael Beye

**Abstract:** Existing calendars, in particular the Gregorian calendar, have a complex structure which requires constant adjustment in the form of leap days and leap seconds in order to remain synchronized to the position of a planet in the Solar System. In addition, mean solar days can deviate up to 16 minutes from true solar days on Earth. On other planets, this discrepancy can be far greater: on Mars, it can be up to 51 minutes. Numerous Martian proposals ranging from Aitken (1936) to Gangale (1986) to Silin (2025) mimic the Gregorian calendar and do not address these issues. Moreover, many do not include a system for telling the time of day, or stretch the second beyond the standard SI second. This paper proposes a calendar mechanism that uses years and days with a variable duration, in order to accurately represent true solar years and days. In this mechanism, time of day is expressed as a proportion, rather than a number of fixed units, such as hours and minutes. This results in a simple and consistent structure that can universally be applied to any planet, moon or space station with a human presence. Furthermore, it allows analog missions on Earth to use the same calendar mechanism as future missions in space. This paper will outline the principles and details of this mechanism, using Earth and Mars as illustrative examples. Also discussed is the implementation of the mechanism on actual computers, which have a Unix time system clock based on discrete milliseconds.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 3

## 125. [Demographic dynamics of the Japanese astronomy and astrophysics publication community during 1996-2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20357) <a id="link125"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.20357

**Authors:** Hideaki Fujiwara

**Abstract:** We investigate the demographic evolution of the Japanese astronomy and astrophysics publication community during 1996-2025 using bibliometric data from 23,274 publications in major journals in the field. The number of active Japan-affiliated authors increased from approximately 600 in the late 1990s to more than 2,000 by the early 2020s, while annual publication output reached approximately 1,400 by 2025. Participation was highly heterogeneous, with 46% of authors observed in only one publication year within the study period and a smaller core showing long observed participation periods. We define the Community Renewal Rate (CRR) as a community-level measure of renewal, calculated as the fraction of active authors not observed during a specified preceding interval. The 5-year CRR typically remained near one quarter of the active community, with annual values ranging from approximately 21% to 33%, indicating persistent renewal despite substantial community growth. An order-of-magnitude comparison suggests that newly entering students account for only part of the renewal-author population. The community therefore expanded while sustaining both continuity through authors with long observed participation periods and renewal through recurring changes in active membership.

## 126. [Eclipse-minimising sun-synchronous orbits in a mean-Sun rotating frame](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19726) <a id="link126"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2611.19726

**Authors:** Kyle Cooper, Yanni Nikas

**Abstract:** A satellite in low Earth orbit normally passes through Earth's shadow on every revolution. A dawn-dusk sun-synchronous orbit largely avoids it: J2 precession keeps the orbit plane facing the Sun all year, but it follows only the Sun's average position, the mean Sun. In reality the Sun wanders up to 23 degrees around this average, enough to push the plane into shadow for part of the year. We introduce MSSI, a coordinate frame that rotates with the mean Sun. In it, a sun-synchronous orbit is a fixed ring and the real Sun traces the analemma, so the yearly eclipse question becomes static geometry. From that picture we derive how much of each orbit is in shadow, the yearly eclipse for every plane orientation, maps of which directions are never, sometimes or always eclipsed, and the margin a mission has. Fixed planes become eclipse-free above about 1400 km, within a window of node offsets that widens with altitude, and the 06:00 and 18:00 lines turn out to be equivalent. The closed form is checked against a full-force-model numerical propagation and against the published eclipse seasons of three flying missions; the model, its code and an interactive 3-D visualizer are published as open source.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 3

---

# Paper Selection Prompt

## System Prompt

> You are a helpful paper reading assistant whose job is to read daily posts from ArXiv and rank the papers in order according to your friend's interests.
> Your job is to carefully read the paper titles and abstracts below and determine how well they match the criteria below.

## User Prompt

> ## Instructions
> 
> Write the response in JSONL format with {ARXIVID, COMMENT, RELEVANCE, NOVELTY} on each line, one for each paper.
> 
> - ARXIVID: should be the ArXiv ID.
> - COMMENT: for papers with RELEVANCE 7-10, briefly identify the specific matching criterion in at most 10 words. For papers with RELEVANCE 1-6, use an empty string. Never explain why a paper is irrelevant.
> - RELEVANCE: should be a score from 1-10.
> - NOVELTY: should be a score from 1-10.
> 
> 
> ## Scoring Criteria
> 
> > The "Relevance" score measures how closely the paper aligns with the core topics of the prompt.
> > The "Novelty" score assesses the originality and impact of the paper.
> > They are two **ORTHONORMAL** axes and **SHOULD NOT** be confused with each other.
> > When determining the final ranking of papers, the relevance score should be given twice as much weight as the novelty score.
> 
> ### Relevance Scoring
> 
> - Relevance 9-10 (Completely Relevant)
>   - Focus: Fully aligned with core topics with no deviation, score the highest if contains relevant keywords in it.
>   - Examples: Papers focused on foundational methods or theoretical research whose topic directly relates to core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 7-8 (Relevant)
>   - Focus: Retain a solid link to the main research area, though may touch on peripheral elements.
>   - Examples: Papers whose topics generally align with the core topics, but may other aspects or applications not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Focus: Maintains a link to the core topics but also extends into at least one other domain/area beyond the primary focus.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results have implications for the core topics but are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 3-4 (Irrelevant)
>   - Focus: Largely outside our interests with only very weak association to our topics.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results may have limited impact on the core topics and are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 1-2 (Ignore)
>   - Focus: Purely unrelated to our topics. Completely a different domain.
> 
> ### Novelty Scoring
> 
> - Novelty 9-10 (Breakthrough)
>   - Definition: Groundbreaking methods/theory/application introducing new directions or solving major challenges.
>   - Examples: Entirely new paradigm for core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 7-8 (Improvements)
>   - Definition: Substantial insights/enhancements, though not a full paradigm shift.
>   - Examples: Modifications on existing methods yielding significantly better results. Applications to new datasets that directly address the core topics. Re-analysis of old datasets using new methods that directly address the core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Definition: Incremental contributions with possible long-term benefits, not immediately transformative.
> 
> - Novelty 3-4 (Tangential)
>   - Definition: Minor or domain-specific improvements with limited broader impact.
> 
> - Novelty 1-2 (Low)
>   - Definition: Methods/application not applicable to core topics.
> 
> 
> ## Papers
> 
> [PAPER LIST HERE]
> 
> ## Relevant Topics
> 
> Use the following relevance criteria when considering which papers to keep. To be rated as highly relevant, a paper must fit at least one of the relevant topics below. Papers which fit multiple categories should be rated more highly.
> 
> 1. Near-Field Cosmology
>    - Relevant: Astrophysics work focusing on dwarf satellite galaxies of the Milky Way, extragalactic satellite luminosity functions, measurements of dark matter density profiles (core-cusp), abundances of dwarf galaxies, signs of historic interactions between the Milky Way and other galaxies (e.g., the accreted stellar halo). Both theoretical and observational work is relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on Milky Way properties like the stellar disk, bar, and Galactic center. Papers that focus on higher mass galaxies, like massive spirals and elliptical galaxies.
> 
> 2. Resolved Star Formation Histories
>    - Relevant: Observational work deriving star formation histories from observed color-magnitude diagrams of galaxies. Theoretical work measuring star formation histories of simulated galaxies is also relevant, but should be weighted lower.
> 
> 3. Star Formation Quenching
>    - Relevant: Observational work discussing the quenching of star formation in galaxies, **particularly dwarf galaxies**. Work on higher mass galaxies may still be relevant, but should be weighted lower. Theoretical work on the quenching of star formation of dwarf galaxies is also relevant.
> 
> 4. Stellar Models
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new stellar models, either stellar evolutionary tracks (i.e., stellar interior models) or stellar atmosphere models. Work presenting new tables of bolometric corrections is also relevant.
> 
> 5. Numerical Galaxy Simulations
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new numerical simulations of galaxies is relevant, especially high-resolution simulations. Simulations that include new physics models (e.g., ionizing radiation, cosmic ray transport) should be given increased weight. Work analyzing previously published simulations should be weighted lower, unless the work also pertains to one of the other relevant topics. Semi-analytic work on galaxies that matches the other criteria is also relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Numerical simulations that do not focus on galaxies and do not fulfill other criteria are not relevant. Examples include simulations of tidal disruption events, black hole accretion disks, and planet formation.
> 
> 6. Star Formation Driven Outflows
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the role the star formation processes play on driving galaxy-scale gas outflows. Measurements of mass-loading factors are particularly relevant. Work commenting on the phase structure of the outflows is also relevant. Work on galaxies more massive than the LMC should be weighted lower.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on outflows driven by sources other than stellar feedback, such as AGN-driven feedback. Papers are relevant if they include discussion of both star-formation-driven outflows and other types of outflows.
> 
> 7. Circumgalactic Medium
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the circumgalactic media (CGM) of dwarf galaxies is relevant. Increased weight should be given to work that connects the CGM to ISM outflows from the galaxy.
> 
> 
> 
> **Keywords:**
> 
> - Relevant: Near-field cosmology, satellite luminosity function, core-cusp, substructure, resolved star formation history, satellite quenching, mass-loading factor
> - Irrelevant: bar, Galactic center, tidal disruption event (TDE), supermassive black hole (SMBH)
